# B30-13, chunk one: what actually produces the number, on the one industry where the whole chain is documented

**Read 2026-08-30. This is a descriptive collection and not a test**, and it is
labelled as one throughout. **The evidence was in hand before any criterion was
written**, so nothing here scores against a preregistration. §4.4 of the design file
fixes the criteria for the arm that would test something, before the next
collection.

**The question that generated it**: does the maker compute the number, or does it
find out the car does not sell and override by hand?

## Layer one: the guide price is produced by working backwards from the competitive set

**Target costing.** The industry's own standard method, documented in the accounting
literature and taught as automotive practice. The order of operations is:

```
market price the competitive set supports   <- input, taken from the neighbours
minus target margin
= allowable cost                            <- output, engineered to
```

**Note what this does to the textbook order.** In the textbook, cost is exogenous
and price is what comes out of cost meeting demand. **In the industry's own
description of its own method, price is the exogenous input and cost is what gets
solved for.** The arrow is reversed, and it is reversed by the practitioners rather
than by this project's reading of them.

## Layer two: the maker does not set the transacted price, and the transacted price is below cost

中国汽车流通协会, 2025 first half, dealer panel:

| quantity | value |
|---|---|
| dealers with 价格倒挂, transacted price below acquisition cost | **74.4%** |
| of those, inversion depth above 15 per cent | **43.6%** |
| **新车销售的毛利贡献率** | **-22.3%** |
| 售后 gross margin contribution | 63.8% |
| 金融保险 gross margin contribution | 36.2% |
| loss-making dealers, 2025 H1 | 52.6% |
| profitable dealers, full-year 2025 | **23.5%** |

**The car itself is a loss line for three quarters of the population selling it.**
The margin is entirely elsewhere. That is [`b28`](b28_results.md)'s non-price margin
at industry scale, with the trade association's own arithmetic rather than this
project's.

## Layer three: the mechanism that makes selling below cost rational, and the part that matters most

返利. 流通协会副秘书长郎学红:

> *"厂家对汽车经销商的返利周期集中在2—3个月，部分实施季度考核的厂家返利周期超过3个月。"*

The rebate splits into 基础返利 and **模糊返利**, and the load-bearing item is a
dealer principal's statement about the second: 模糊返利占比过高, with the consequence
that

> *"经销商对实得返利很难精准计算"*

**So the party quoting the number cannot compute its own margin at the moment it
quotes.** Not "does not bother to". Cannot, and **the opacity is the counterparty's
design**, since the maker chooses how much of the rebate is 模糊.

**This is worth stating plainly because of what it does to the standard
construction.** Every derivation of a supply curve requires the seller to know its
own marginal cost. **Here the seller provably does not, across 74.4 per cent of the
population, by a structure the other party built on purpose.** On this carrier a
supply curve is not merely unmeasured. **It is not defined.**

## The three layers together

The number a Chinese Audi buyer pays is produced by: a guide price taken from the
competitive set by working backwards from it, minus a discount chosen by a dealer
who is selling below its own cost and cannot compute its own rebate, against a
volume target set two to three months out.

**No step in that chain contains a demand curve, an elasticity estimate, or a
market-clearing computation.**

And the observable maker decision, the 10.49万 官降 of 2026-03-25, arrives at a
granularity of **one decision per model generation**, after monthly volume had
already collapsed. **That is the override, and it is dated.**

**Direct answer.** The maker has a cost model and a competitive-positioning process.
**It has no model whose output is the transacted price.** What outputs the
transacted price is a rebate schedule meeting a volume target at a counter.

## The A6L episode, recorded as an application rather than as a new result

The chain's older conclusions already cover it, and it is filed as an instance.
The maker put the implied level up, met resistance, and cut. **Market force acted
through "it stopped selling", and not through any party learning a supply and demand
condition.** And by Theorem 1 the adjustment cannot produce a single price even if
executed perfectly, because the cycles still do not close: the same repositioning
left **1.5万 of regional dispersion three months later.**

## What this chunk establishes and what it does not

**Established.**

1. **The documented method for setting the posted number takes price as input and
   produces cost as output.**
2. **The transacted number is set by a party that is not the maker, below its own
   acquisition cost, on 74.4 per cent of the population.**
3. **That party cannot compute its own margin when it quotes**, by the maker's
   design.
4. **The maker's own correction arrives once per model generation, after a volume
   signal.**

**Not established.**

1. **No internal Audi document.** Everything here is industry-level. **Whether Audi
   runs conjoint or willingness-to-pay work at product planning is not established
   and it very likely does.** The claim is about what produces the transacted
   number, not about whether quantitative work exists anywhere in the firm.
2. **That this generalises past autos.** §4.4 registers the test and names the
   likely counter-carrier.

## Limits

1. **The association's figures are a self-reported panel of its own members**, one
   body, and the reporting incentive around losses is not neutral.
2. **Target costing is documented as the industry's standard practice**, and it is
   not a statement by Audi about Audi.
3. **2025 dealer arithmetic against a 2026 decision.** The layer-two figures are a
   year older than the repositioning they are used to explain.
4. **The 模糊返利 statement is one dealer principal quoted by one outlet**, carried
   by the association's official in the same piece. It is the single most
   load-bearing sentence in this chunk and it rests on that.

---

# B30-1 and B30-5, chunk two: the ATPCO record read against the registered criterion

**Read 2026-08-30** against the criterion registered for this arm
the same day, **before the record was pulled**.

> **The registered criterion.** Can the public record yield pairs of the form
> "A initiated, B followed on day N", with N countable? **If not, the carrier is
> void and the negative is recorded on the spot.**

**Strict answer: no. N is not countable from the public record, and the criterion
fails as written.** What the record supplies instead is worth more than what the
criterion asked for. The rest of this chunk says exactly what, and why the carrier
is kept rather than voided.

## What the record does supply

### The adjacency, authored by both parties and entered into a court record

DOJ Competitive Impact Statement, *United States v. Airline Tariff Publishing Co.*,
complaint filed **1992-12-21**, eight airline defendants (Alaska, American,
Continental, Delta, Northwest, TWA, United, USAir) plus ATP.

The worked episode, **April 1989, Dallas-Chicago**:

> *"In April 1989, American offered certain discount fares between its hubs in
> Dallas and Chicago on a few select flights on that route each day."*
>
> *"American matched Delta's action by filing the discount fares on all of its
> flights in Dallas-Chicago, but it added a last ticket date to those fares of only
> a few days away."*
>
> *"American also filed fares between Dallas and Atlanta, two of Delta's hubs, using
> the same fare levels, footnote designator and last ticket date."*
>
> *"A Delta pricing employee, observing the same dollar amounts and footnotes on
> American's fares in the two city pairs, noted that American's fares in
> Dallas-Atlanta were an 'obvious retaliation.'"*

Outcome recited: the arrangement *"raised the price of a roundtrip ticket between
Dallas and Chicago by as much as $138 for many travellers."*

**This meets B29-3's hard condition at the highest quality this project has seen.**
Who watches whom is not inferred by the analyst. **Both parties wrote it down, in
their own internal documents, and a court received them.**

**Scale.** Over fifty agreements, hundreds of city pairs, *"from heavily travelled
business markets such as New York--Chicago to smaller leisure travel markets such as
Klamath Falls, Oregon--Tampa, Florida"*, over a period running *"from at least as
early as April 1988 and continuing through at least May 1990."*

### The propagation procedure, in the industry's own operating practice

- ATP compiles all industry fare change information and distributes it **at least
  once a day**.
- **First ticket dates let a carrier announce a change ahead of implementation.**
- Carriers *"iterated back and forth until they reached a point where they were
  announcing the same fare increase to take effect on the same date."*
- And the rule that governs whether a number survives:

> *"when one airline did not announce that it would post the same fare increase as
> the others, the increase generally did not take place"*, with the dates rolled
> forward.

**That sentence is this chain's propagation claim written out in the parties' own
procedure.** A number is posted; the neighbours register objection **by not
matching**; the number is withdrawn or rolled. **The price that survives is the one
that survived an objection round.** Nothing in the procedure requires any party to
have learned anything about demand, and **the record contains no such learning.**

### Why N is not countable

The daily fare-change files were **evidence, not publication**. The Competitive
Impact Statement gives **month resolution** on the worked episode and no lag
distribution at all. **The case never went to trial**, so the transactional record
was never published, and Borenstein's account of it reports patterns rather than
dated pairs for the same reason.

**Recorded as a negative on the criterion as written.**

## What replaces it, and why it is a better instrument than the one asked for

**The decree is a dated deletion of edges in the information graph only.**

Final judgment covering **United and USAir, term ten years from 1993-11-01**,
Section IV enjoins them from:

| section | prohibition, verbatim |
|---|---|
| IV.2 | *"disseminating any first ticket dates, last ticket dates, or any other information concerning"* contemplated fare changes |
| IV.4 | *"making visible or disseminating any fare that is intended solely to communicate a defendant's planned or contemplated fares"* |
| IV.5 | *"making visible or disseminating two or more footnote designators that identify footnotes that contain identical information"* |
| IV.6 | *"using fare codes that convey information other than fare class or terms and conditions"* |

Section V leaves permitted: submitting fare changes to ATP without first or last
ticket dates, advertising promotional fares with cessation dates in general
circulation media, **promptly matching a promotional fare after a competitor
disseminates one**, and public statements without specific effective dates or
amounts.

**Read that against [`b30_propagation.md`](b30_propagation.md) §6.** The two-graph
split says transport runs on `G_I`, falsification runs on cycles of `G_T`, and the
part supported on `G_I \ G_T` is unfalsifiable.

**This decree deletes edges lying exactly in `G_I \ G_T` and leaves `G_T`
untouched.** No route was closed, no aircraft moved, no fare level barred, and
matching itself stays legal. **Only the announcement channel was cut.**

**A third party who is neither buyer nor seller changed the information graph on a
date and left the trade graph alone.** The project has wanted that operator since
B30-8, where it appeared as edge **creation** in the trade graph. **Here it is edge
deletion in the information graph, court-ordered, with the instruments named section
by section.**

### And the treatment is staggered

United and USAir from **1993-11-01**. The other six from the proposed final judgment
of **1994-03-17**, **entered 1994-08-10** (corrected 2026-08-30 from Miller's own
recital: *"United and US Airways settled on November 1, 1993, followed by Alaska,
American, Continental, Delta, Northwest, and Trans World Airlines on August 10,
1994"*). **The binding date is entry, so the staggered window is about nine months,
not four and a half, and the clean quarters are 1994Q1 and 1994Q2.**
**The two decrees differ in stringency**, and the government's own
filing says so:

> Section V(C) *"permits the airline defendants to disseminate last ticket dates
> through ATP in some specified circumstances where the United/USAir decree prohibits
> the use of last ticket dates."*

**So there is a window of roughly four and a half months in which two named carriers
had lost the announcement instruments while six had not**, followed by a period in
which all eight were covered but two more strictly.

**That window is B30-1's discriminating cell, delivered by a court instead of by
nature**: nodes with no direct access to the signal against nodes with direct
access, on a date, with the deletion authored, published, and enumerated.

## The prior result, disclosed before anything is registered on it

**Rule 8a.** Miller (2010), *Did the Airline Tariff Publishing Case Reduce
Collusion?*, Journal of Law and Economics 53(3), ran a version of this, and **I read
her abstract before writing any criterion for it.** Verbatim:

> *"Prices fell in response to the investigation, but increased following the
> settlement, while the number of tickets sold in affected markets declined. The
> importance of multi-market contact also dropped and then recovered. The ATP case
> had at best a temporary effect on airline collusion."*

**So the direction of the level effect is known to me and no arm may claim
out-of-sample credit for it.** What remains genuinely unseen is registered in the
design file §4.6: **Miller measured price levels and multimarket contact, and this
chain's quantity is neither.**

## What this chunk establishes

1. **The registered criterion fails on its strict form**, and the failure is recorded
   rather than reinterpreted into a pass.
2. **The carrier supplies party-authored adjacency at the highest quality this
   project has**, on a litigated public record, with named nodes and a worked
   episode.
3. **The industry's own procedure is post, wait for objection, withdraw or roll**,
   with the government's sentence supplying the objection rule.
4. **The 1993 and 1994 decrees are a dated, staggered, third-party deletion of edges
   lying entirely in `G_I \ G_T`**, which is the intervention the two-graph split
   needed and had never had.

## Limits

1. **One worked episode, at month resolution.** Everything about lag structure is
   absent.
2. **The Competitive Impact Statement is the government's allegation.** The case
   settled without any adjudication of the facts, and the defendants admitted
   nothing.
3. **The decree text was read section by section from DOJ's own page.** The two
   decrees were not compared line by line and only the V(C) difference is used.
4. **Miller's paper: abstract only.** Her data, method and controls have not been
   examined. **Any arm built next to hers must read the paper first**, and §4.6 says
   so as a precondition rather than as a nicety.

# B30-12, chunk four: a maker that prices off the secondary market, and posts two numbers for one bottle

**Read 2026-08-30.** Registered under §4.3's D-table. **This case supplies the
traversal between two cells of that table, with dates**, and it supplies the
anchoring direction asserted by the seller about itself.

## The numbers, one product, one seller, one day

飞天茅台, as reported for **2026-08-08**:

| channel | posted | who owns the channel |
|---|---|---|
| **线下自营店** | **1,753 yuan** | the maker |
| **i茅台**, the maker's own app | **1,639 yuan** | the maker |
| gap | **114 yuan** | |
| 散瓶批发价 | about 1,696 yuan | wholesale |
| 市场终端实际成交均价 | about **1,750 yuan** | terminal transactions |

And the history of the posted number:

- **1,499 yuan**, the long-standing 官方指导零售价.
- **2026-03-31**, the maker raised its own 自营渠道 price to **1,539 yuan**.
- **2026-08-08**, its own stores are at 1,753.

## What the reporting says the two prices are anchored to

The characterisation given is that **i茅台锚定批发流通价** while **线下自营店锚定终端真实
成交价**, with the offline price described as carrying 房租、库存、即时交付、现场验真
service costs that the app price does not.

**Read the direction of that sentence.** The maker's own posted numbers are described
as anchoring **to observed market prices**, one to wholesale and one to terminal
transactions. **The causal arrow points from the secondary market into the
manufacturer's price**, which is the reverse of the direction every textbook
construction assumes, and it is asserted about the manufacturer rather than inferred
about it.

**Flagged before it is used: the 锚定 phrasing is the reporting's, not a quoted
company statement.** It is treated as a third-party characterisation, and the arm
does not rest on it. **The numbers do not depend on it.**

## Three readings, in decreasing order of how much they rest on

**1. Two posted numbers for one identical bottle, from the same seller, on the same
day, with a substitution-class reason given.** No intermediary, no transport, no
scarcity difference, no bargaining. **The stated reason is that the two channels are
not selling the same thing**, because one bundles rent, stock, immediate handover and
on-the-spot authentication.

**This is the same operator as Costco's self-edge refusal** recorded in the quotation
graph chunk, where a seller declined in writing to reconcile its own two prices on
the ground that the channels differ. **Different jurisdiction, different product,
same structure, and here it comes with figures.**

**2. The 1,499 number was a 表态价 and its retirement is dated.** For years the posted
number sat far below what anyone paid, and the poster held it. Then on **2026-03-31**
the poster moved its own number, and by August its own stores were 17 per cent above
that. **So this carrier traversed §4.3's table from `D > 1, 不动` to
`任意, 动向成交价`, and the traversal has a date on it.**

**3. The traversal direction is the opposite of the A6L's and the mechanism is the
same.** The A6L's posted number sat **above** the transacted one and came **down** to
meet it. 茅台's sat **below** and went **up** to meet it. **In both cases the posted
number moved to the transacted number, and in neither case did the transacted number
move to the posted one.** §4.3's fourth row is now filled from both directions.

## What this chunk establishes and what it does not

**Established.**

1. **A single seller posting two different numbers for one identical good on one
   day, through two channels it owns outright**, with a stated reason that is a
   substitution-class assertion.
2. **A dated retirement of a long-held posted number**, and the direction of the
   move.
3. **Both directions of §4.3's fourth row**, from one product family each.

**Not established.**

1. **That the maker takes a share of the secondary market.** §4.3 calls that the
   strongest available cell. **What is in hand is the opposite sign**: the maker
   selling at 1,499 through its own app has been described as compressing the
   reseller premium rather than taking a cut of it. **That is a different finding and
   it is not collected here.**
2. **Any transacted-price series.** The terminal average is one figure on one day.

## Limits

1. **One relay report of a single day's prices.** No series.
2. **The 锚定 characterisation is the reporter's**, as flagged above.
3. **The 1,499 to 1,539 to 1,753 sequence mixes two different objects**: 1,499 was
   the 官方指导零售价 for the channel generally, 1,539 was a 自营渠道 price, and 1,753
   is one store's posted price. **They are not the same series and are not treated as
   one.**

# B30-16, chunk one: the cross-carrier fare gap becomes a pair of constants, and the date range matters

**Read 2026-08-30** against the criteria registered for this arm
and §4.8a before the data was pulled. **Carrier: DOT Consumer Airfare Report Table 6,
Socrata API, four first quarters.** Script `experiments/b30_16_rank.py`.

## What is being measured

Table 6 gives, per city pair per quarter, exactly two carriers: the largest and the
lowest-fare, each with its own fare. **With exactly two cells per route the two-way
fixed-effect model `log fare = a_carrier + b_route` reduces exactly to the
within-route difference**, so the route level is differenced out rather than
estimated, and the rank-one prediction stands bare:

```
log fare_lg - log fare_low  =  c_lg - c_low
```

`n_carriers` free parameters against `n_routes` observations.

## The reading, with the composition check run alongside

`prune` drops carriers appearing on fewer than 30 routes, which is the filter stage
one used and stage two originally did not. **Both rows are reported because a rise in
fit could otherwise be a change in who is in the sample.**

| quarter | prune | routes | carriers | pairs | gap sd | **R² rank one** | R² pair | route incr. | route alone | null |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1996Q1 | no | 2769 | 39 | 172 | 0.216 | **0.156** | 0.238 | +0.002 | 0.018 | 0.015 |
| 1996Q1 | **yes** | 2545 | 12 | 81 | 0.210 | **0.110** | 0.166 | +0.004 | 0.014 | 0.004 |
| 2005Q1 | no | 3412 | 20 | 134 | 0.164 | **0.055** | 0.160 | +0.014 | 0.017 | 0.005 |
| 2005Q1 | **yes** | 3378 | 15 | 126 | 0.161 | **0.036** | 0.144 | +0.014 | 0.016 | 0.004 |
| 2015Q1 | no | 3208 | 12 | 60 | 0.181 | **0.455** | 0.488 | +0.008 | 0.113 | 0.004 |
| 2015Q1 | **yes** | 3177 | 9 | 52 | 0.173 | **0.411** | 0.441 | +0.009 | 0.108 | 0.002 |
| 2024Q1 | no | 2136 | 13 | 59 | 0.263 | **0.713** | 0.733 | +0.002 | 0.131 | 0.005 |
| 2024Q1 | **yes** | 2096 | 9 | 44 | 0.250 | **0.694** | 0.713 | +0.003 | 0.140 | 0.003 |

## Four readings

**1. The registered criterion on route characteristics is met in all eight rows.**
Adding log distance and log passengers on top of the rank-one model raises R² by
**0.002 to 0.014**, and those features alone explain **0.014 to 0.140**. **Route-specific
pricing does not explain the gap in any period**, which is what §4.8a registered as
the pass condition for that half.

**2. The rank-one fit itself is not stable, and the design did not anticipate that.**
It runs **0.110, 0.036, 0.411, 0.694** across the four quarters. **So the rank-one
reading fails in 1996 and 2005 and holds in 2015 and 2024**, and that is recorded as a
split verdict rather than resolved either way.

**3. The rise is not a degrees-of-freedom artifact, and the direction rules it out.**
The rank-one model's parameter count is the carrier count. **1996 used 12 parameters
for 0.110; 2024 used 9 for 0.694.** Fewer parameters, six times the fit. A
df artifact runs the other way.

**4. It is not a sample-composition artifact either, and that was checked rather
than argued.** Pruning small carriers **lowers** R² slightly in every quarter
(0.156 to 0.110, 0.055 to 0.036, 0.455 to 0.411, 0.713 to 0.694) **and leaves the time
pattern intact.** So the small carriers were not the source of the early period's
poor fit. **The check was run because stage one and stage two disagreed on the
carrier count, and the disagreement was mine.**

**And the dispersion itself did not shrink.** Gap sd is 0.216, 0.164, 0.181, 0.263.
**What changed is the structure, not the size.**

## What the split verdict says in words

**In 1996 the same pair of carriers charged very different relative amounts on
different routes. In 2024, knowing which two carriers are involved predicts about
seven tenths of the gap.** The consolidation from 39 observed carriers to 13 sits
between the two.

## The limit that matters most, stated before anyone builds on this

**Rank deficiency shows the quotes are informationally thin. It does not by itself
show why.** A world in which carriers have genuinely constant quality or cost
differentials produces the same signature as a world in which they copy a shared
positional convention. **Discriminator one cannot separate those two and was never
claimed to.**

**What would separate them** is whether the estimated carrier constants track
measurable quality and cost differences. If the constants are real differentials
they should move with on-time performance, seat pitch, and fuel cost per available
seat mile. If they are a convention they should not. **That is registered as the
next chunk rather than asserted here.**

## Other limits

1. **Two carriers per route, and they are selected on the outcome.** The lowest-fare
   carrier is defined by having the lowest fare. The log difference cancels the route
   level so the rank-one prediction survives this, but **the pair composition is not
   random and is likely correlated with distance and market size**, so every R² here
   is conditional on the observed pair.
2. **Table 6 covers only markets averaging at least ten passengers a day.** Thin
   routes are absent by construction.
3. **Four quarters, all first quarters, is not a time series.** The 1996 to 2024
   movement is four points and no intermediate years were read.
4. **No DB1B cross-check yet.** DB1B carries every carrier on every route and would
   remove limit 1 entirely. 1993Q1, 1994Q1 and 1995Q1 are downloaded and unread.
5. **Ten shuffles per cell.** Enough here because the excess over the null is one to
   two orders of magnitude larger than the null itself in every row.

# B30-16, chunk two: DB1B with no carrier selection, and a correction to chunk one's trend

**Read 2026-08-30.** Carrier: BTS DB1BMarket, 1993Q1, 1994Q1, 1995Q1, pulled through
the form. **Every carrier on every route, 4.30 to 4.57 per route, selected by nobody**,
which is what removes chunk one's first limit.

Route basis is the DOT city market, matching Table 6. Rows with the ticketing carrier
changing inside the market are dropped. **That filter turned out to bind on nothing:
`TK_CARRIER_CHANGE` and `OP_CARRIER_CHANGE` are zero on every row in these three
files.** It is kept because later years may differ.

| quarter | rows scanned | kept | cells | routes | carriers | per route |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1993Q1 | 2,798,543 | 2,223,470 | 9,469 | 2,204 | 11 | 4.30 |
| 1994Q1 | 2,876,398 | 2,268,886 | 9,672 | 2,201 | 13 | 4.39 |
| 1995Q1 | 3,143,532 | 2,533,742 | 9,794 | 2,141 | 15 | 4.57 |

## The statistic, and why it has no degrees-of-freedom problem

With three or more carriers per route, **subtracting the route mean absorbs the route
fixed effect exactly.** So the reported R² carries `n_carriers - 1` parameters against
about 9,500 observations, rather than `n_carriers + n_routes`.

**Route characteristics cannot enter directly, because they are constant within a
route and vanish under demeaning.** The analogue registered in §4.8a is the carrier
by route interaction: does a carrier's premium depend on how big the route is, or on
how many rivals are on it. **Under rank one it does not.**

## The reading

| quarter | within-route sd of log fare | **R² carrier constants** | + carrier × log route pax | + carrier × rivals on route | + both | shuffled null |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1993Q1 | 0.1723 | **0.4126** | +0.0160 | +0.0135 | +0.0243 | 0.0015 |
| 1994Q1 | 0.1739 | **0.3574** | +0.0163 | +0.0112 | +0.0233 | 0.0015 |
| 1995Q1 | 0.1911 | **0.4198** | +0.0080 | +0.0107 | +0.0176 | 0.0022 |

**Carrier constants explain 36 to 42 per cent of within-route log fare variation, and
the null is 0.0015 to 0.0022**, so the excess is about two hundred times the null.

**The registered criterion passes cleanly.** Letting each carrier's premium vary with
route size adds **0.8 to 1.6 points**; letting it vary with the number of rivals adds
**1.1 to 1.4**; both together add **1.8 to 2.4**. **A carrier's position relative to
the others on a route does not meaningfully depend on how big the route is or on how
many rivals are standing there.**

## The correction to chunk one, and it is a correction to my own comparison

**Chunk one read a rise from 0.11 in 1996 to 0.69 in 2024 and treated it as a change
in pricing structure. The DB1B numbers above sit at 0.36 to 0.42 in 1993 to 1995,
which is the 2015 level, three decades early.** That is the shape of a statistic
problem, not of a discovery.

**So the two statistics were rebuilt on the same data rather than argued about.**
Table 6's is a within-route log gap between exactly **two** carriers, both picked on
the outcome (largest by passengers, lowest by fare). **That selection was reproduced
out of the DB1B cells and Table 6's regression run on it:**

| quarter | routes | carriers | pairs | mean log gap | **R² rank one, Table 6 statistic** | R² pair | route incr. | null |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1993Q1 | 1,727 | 11 | 80 | 0.283 | **0.176** | 0.233 | +0.066 | 0.006 |
| 1994Q1 | 1,710 | 13 | 88 | 0.274 | **0.222** | 0.284 | +0.050 | 0.008 |
| 1995Q1 | 1,756 | 15 | 111 | 0.324 | **0.210** | 0.279 | +0.042 | 0.009 |

**Same years, same source, two statistics: 0.41 with every carrier, 0.20 with Table
6's two selected ones.** The extreme-order pair is intrinsically less explainable by
carrier constants. **So roughly half the apparent gap between the chunks is the
statistic, and chunk one's cross-chunk comparison was mine to get wrong.**

## And the trend survives, on one constant statistic

Putting only Table-6-statistic numbers in one column:

```
1993  0.176   DB1B
1994  0.222   DB1B
1995  0.210   DB1B
1996  0.156   Table 6
2005  0.055   Table 6
2015  0.455   Table 6
2024  0.713   Table 6
```

**Two things fall out.**

1. **1993 to 1996 agree across two independent data sources** built by different
   pipelines, 0.176, 0.222, 0.210 against 0.156. **That is a pipeline validation that
   was not designed for and it is worth more than the agreement itself.**
2. **The rise from the mid-1990s to 2024 is not a statistic artifact.** Held on one
   statistic it runs 0.18 to 0.71, with a dip at 2005.

**What chunk one claimed still stands. What has changed is that it now rests on a
comparison that was checked instead of assumed.**

## Limits

1. **Three quarters, all first quarters, all consecutive.** The DB1B leg is 1993 to
   1995 and nothing later has been pulled.
2. **The route-feature discrepancy between the two sources is real and is not the
   feature set.** Distance was carried through on 2026-08-30 and the cells rebuilt
   under a new filter key. **Adding log distance beside log route passengers moved the
   increment by about one thousandth**: +0.0662, +0.0511, +0.0440 against +0.0662,
   +0.0497, +0.0425 before. **So the debt is closed and it was not the cause.**

   What remains is a genuine disagreement between the sources for adjacent years.
   Route features **alone** explain **0.078 to 0.110** of the two-carrier gap in DB1B
   1993 to 1995, against **0.018 and 0.017** in Table 6 1996 and 2005. Candidates,
   none checked: the route populations differ (Table 6 keeps markets averaging ten
   passengers a day, this keeps routes with three or more carriers and twenty sampled
   passengers a cell); the largest carrier is DOT-computed there and sampled-passenger
   computed here; Table 6 fares are DOT-processed and these are raw MARKET_FARE.
   **Recorded as unresolved. It does not touch the rank-one numbers, which agree
   across the two sources at 0.176 to 0.222 against 0.156.**
3. **The 2005 dip is unexplained and rests on one quarter of one source.**
4. **Everything in both chunks is R² on within-route variation.** Rank deficiency
   shows the quotes are informationally thin and does not say why, per chunk one's
   main limit, which is unchanged.

# B30-17, chunk one: the Treasury curve returns a negative on its own registered criteria, and the negative is against this project's tool

**Read 2026-08-30** against the criteria registered for this arm
**before any data was pulled.** Carrier: Treasury daily par yield curve (CMT), free,
two windows. Script `experiments/b30_17_curve.py`.

**Publication grid: rates print to two decimals in percent, so one basis point.**
Uniform rounding gives a floor sd of `1/sqrt(12) = 0.2887` bp on levels and
`sqrt(2)/sqrt(12) = 0.4082` bp on daily changes. **That is D24's resolution floor as a
measured number, and it is what this arm was built to use.**

Balanced panel, eleven maturities: 1M, 3M, 6M, 1Y, 2Y, 3Y, 5Y, 7Y, 10Y, 20Y, 30Y.
The 2-month (from 2018-10-16) and 4-month (from 2022-10-19) fall below the coverage
threshold and are excluded, which is itself the column-addition question and is not
touched here.

## The reading, both windows

| | | 2010 to 2024, 3752 days | | 2016 to 2024, 2250 days | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| | k | cum var | resid / floor | cum var | resid / floor |
| **LEVELS** | 1 | 0.9049 | 147.8 | 0.9630 | 101.3 |
| | 2 | 0.9835 | 61.5 | 0.9917 | 47.8 |
| | **3** | **0.9975** | **23.8** | **0.9985** | **20.4** |
| | 6 | 0.99982 | 6.4 | 0.99984 | 6.6 |
| | 10 | 0.999993 | 1.25 | 0.999996 | 1.05 |
| **DAILY CHANGES** | 1 | 0.7392 | 5.74 | 0.7153 | 6.49 |
| | **3** | **0.9241** | **3.10** | **0.9205** | **3.43** |
| | 7 | 0.9906 | 1.09 | 0.9919 | 1.09 |
| | **8** | **0.9945** | **0.83** | **0.9953** | **0.84** |

Shuffled null at three factors: 0.454 and 0.389 on the long window, 0.448 and 0.354 on
the short one. **The excess over the null is large and is not what fails here.**

**The two windows agree to two or three decimals throughout, so the zero-rate era is
not driving any of this.**

## The verdict, which is a negative on the registered criteria

§4.9's third branch: **more than six factors needed to reach 99 per cent means
discriminator one fails on this carrier.** On daily changes, 99 per cent is first
reached at **k = 7**. **That branch fires and the arm returns a negative.** It is
recorded here rather than resolved by reaching for the branch that would have passed.

**And the negative runs against this project's own tool, which is why it is worth
more than a pass would have been.**

## What actually happened, in three parts

**1. The benchmark was not reproduced on the quantity it was cited for, and the
citation was loose.** The chain quoted "three factors explain more than 99 per cent of
Treasury yield movement" as a pipeline validation target. **On daily changes three
factors give 92.4 and 92.1 per cent.** On levels they give 99.75 and 99.85, which does
clear 99. **So the folk figure is a levels statement, or a statement about a different
construction, and it was carried into the design without checking which.** That is
mine and it is recorded as such.

**2. The residual against printing precision is what kills the low-rank reading, and
it kills it by a factor of about eight.** Three factors leave **6.87 bp** of residual
on levels against a **0.289 bp** grid, which is **23.8 times the smallest thing the
publication can express.** On changes, three factors leave 3.1 times the floor.
**Getting below the floor takes k = 8 on changes and essentially the full eleven on
levels.**

**So the published curve carries about eight numbers on daily changes, and close to
its full width on levels, at the precision Treasury actually prints.**

**3. Variance share and information content are not the same quantity, and this arm
measured the gap.** "Three factors, 99 per cent" and "the curve carries three numbers"
are different claims. **The first is true on levels. The second is false at the
published precision, by a factor of nearly three on changes and by more on levels.**

## What this does to §1e's discriminator one

**It does not retire it. It supplies the missing half of it.**

§1e-2 already said that without a noise floor "the rank is three" is a choice rather
than a measurement. **This arm is the first place the project has had a floor that is
a measured number, and the first thing that floor did was reject a reading the
variance share would have accepted.**

**So the operational form of discriminator one is now: report the residual against a
stated floor, and never report a variance share alone.** Recorded as failure mode 112.

**And it makes B30-16's numbers weaker, not stronger.** That arm reported R² on
within-route variation with no absolute floor. **Its 0.41 has no floor attached and
therefore cannot support a statement about how many numbers a fare quote carries.**
The airline fare grid is coarser than one basis point, so a floor exists there and was
not used. **B30-16's chunks stand as measurements of variance share and are demoted
from any claim about information content until that floor is computed.**

## Limits

1. **One publisher.** CMT is Treasury's own interpolation, so this measures what the
   published curve carries, not what a multi-party market carries. §1e-2 says the
   cross-party tests do not apply here and they do not.
2. **Low rank is partly guaranteed by construction**, since CMT is an interpolation
   from a smaller input set. **That makes the negative stronger rather than weaker**:
   even an object built by interpolation does not compress to three numbers at its own
   printed precision.
3. **The input set was not collected.** How many on-the-run securities feed the
   interpolation is the genuinely independent question and it is still open.
4. **Two windows, both ending 2024, both containing the 2022 to 2023 rate rise.**
   Nothing before 2010 was pulled.
5. **The column-addition test is not run.** The 2-month and 4-month are excluded by
   coverage here, and whether adding them raises the factor count is §4.9's second
   registered question and remains open.

---

---

# B30-16, chunk three: the floor, measured, and the fare matrix carries exactly one carrier dimension

**Read 2026-08-30** after failure mode 112 demoted chunks one and two from any claim
about information content. **This chunk computes the missing floor and answers the
demoted question.**

## The floor is a standard error, not a printing grid

Treasury prints rates to a grid, so its floor is a rounding sd. **DB1B has no such
grid.** `MARKET_FARE` is a **prorated** fare from a **10 per cent ticket sample**,
averaged into a cell. **So the precision of a cell is the standard error of its own
mean**, and that is computable from the carrier's own data: carry the second moment
through the streamed aggregation, get the within-cell fare sd, divide by the square
root of sampled passengers, and put it on the log scale by the delta method.

| quarter | median sampled pax per cell | **floor, RMS of se on log fare** | median |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1993Q1 | 92 | **0.0641** | 0.0492 |
| 1994Q1 | 101 | **0.0611** | 0.0451 |
| 1995Q1 | 109 | **0.0611** | 0.0438 |

**The floor is about six per cent of the fare.** That is a coarse instrument and it is
the honest one for this carrier.

## Shape, and what it permits asking

The matrix is **11 to 15 carriers by about 2,200 routes**, with **4.3 to 4.6 carriers
observed per route.** Factors live in **carrier** space, so a factor is 11 to 15
numbers and route scores are projections. That is the same well-conditioned situation
as the Treasury panel. **But a route's score vector is estimated from its 4.3 observed
cells, so k above about three is not identified per route and was not attempted.**

## The reading, with a held-out guard that immediately earned its place

Ten per cent of observed cells masked before fitting, three repetitions, residual
reported on the masked cells.

| quarter | k | in-sample resid | **held-out resid** | floor | in / floor | **held-out / floor** |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1993Q1 | **1** | 0.1139 | **0.2146** | 0.0632 | 1.80 | **3.40** |
| 1993Q1 | 2 | 0.0867 | 0.2427 | 0.0632 | 1.37 | 3.84 |
| 1993Q1 | 3 | 0.0617 | 0.2494 | 0.0632 | **0.98** | 3.94 |
| 1994Q1 | **1** | 0.1191 | **0.2400** | 0.0612 | 1.95 | 3.92 |
| 1994Q1 | 3 | 0.0695 | 0.2701 | 0.0612 | 1.14 | 4.41 |
| 1995Q1 | **1** | 0.1287 | **0.2300** | 0.0601 | 2.14 | 3.83 |
| 1995Q1 | 3 | 0.0749 | 0.2648 | 0.0601 | 1.25 | 4.41 |

**Two things, and the first one would have been a false pass.**

**1. In sample, rank three reaches the floor. Held out, rank three is worse than rank
one.** The in-sample residual falls with k by construction; **the held-out residual
rises monotonically in every quarter.** So the apparent arrival at the floor at k = 3
is overfitting, and without the guard it would have been reported as a result.

**2. The held-out residual is minimised at k = 1 in all three quarters.** **The fare
matrix supports exactly one carrier-space dimension, and no more.** That one dimension
is the carrier constant already measured in chunks one and two.

## What the demoted claim becomes

**Failure mode 112 required a floor before any statement about information content.
There is now one, so the statement can be made, and it is narrower than the variance
share suggested.**

> **A quarter of DB1B market fares carries route levels plus one carrier-space
> dimension. After that, the residual is about twice the measurement floor in sample,
> and no second dimension survives a held-out test.**

**The residual is not zero and is not at the floor.** Roughly twice the noise remains
after route level and carrier constant. **So the fare field is not a rank-one object.
It is a rank-one object plus idiosyncratic route-by-carrier variation that this
instrument can see but no low-rank model captures.**

## The comparison with B30-17, and the trap in making it

Treasury daily changes need **k = 8** to fall below their floor. DB1B fares support
**k = 1**. **It is tempting to read that as fares being more convention-bound than
yields. That reading is failure mode 112 again, one level up, and it is refused here.**

**The two floors differ by four orders of magnitude**: 0.4 basis points on a yield
against six per cent of a fare. **A coarse instrument resolves fewer dimensions.**
What is supported is only this:

> **At the precision each carrier's own data affords, the Treasury curve resolves
> about eight dimensions and the airline fare matrix resolves one.**
> **The number of resolvable dimensions is a joint property of the market and of the
> measurement, and any statement that drops the second half is wrong.**

## Limits

1. **The held-out residual is inflated by small-group leave-out variance.** With 4.3
   cells per route, holding one out leaves about 3.3 to estimate the route level, so
   the absolute held-out numbers (3.4 to 4.4 times floor) are not a clean floor
   comparison. **Its shape across k is valid because that inflation is roughly
   constant in k; its level is not.** The in-sample k = 1 figure, 1.8 to 2.1 times
   floor, is the better absolute number.
2. **Three quarters, all first quarters, 1993 to 1995.**
3. **The floor treats sampled passengers as independent draws.** Tickets on one
   itinerary are not independent, so the true standard error is larger and the floor
   here is optimistic, which makes the residual-over-floor ratios upper bounds.
4. **Routes fully masked in a repetition fall back to the global mean.** They are few
   and the warning is recorded rather than suppressed.
5. **B30-17's residuals are in sample.** With 3,752 days against 11 maturities the
   day scores are projections onto an 11-dimensional basis and overfitting is not
   expected, **but the same held-out guard has not been run there and that is a debt.**

---

---

# B30-17, chunk two: every published maturity carries its own dimension, and discriminator one turns out to be much weaker than it looked

**Read 2026-08-30** against §4.9's **second** registered question, which was fixed
before any data was pulled: **adding a column to a rank-k object should not raise k.**

## Part A: can any maturity be predicted from the other ten

Split-sample, five folds, so the answer is a prediction and not a fit. Residual in
basis points against the publication floor.

| maturity | levels, resid bp | × floor | changes, resid bp | × floor |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **1 M** | 10.09 | **34.9** | 4.19 | **10.3** |
| 3 M | 4.52 | 15.7 | 1.98 | 4.8 |
| 6 M | 3.24 | 11.2 | 1.48 | 3.6 |
| 1 Y | 3.16 | 10.9 | 1.65 | 4.0 |
| 2 Y | 2.41 | 8.4 | 1.42 | 3.5 |
| 3 Y | 2.03 | 7.0 | 1.10 | 2.7 |
| **5 Y** | **1.71** | **5.9** | 0.84 | 2.1 |
| 7 Y | 1.94 | 6.7 | 0.74 | **1.8** |
| 10 Y | 2.90 | 10.1 | 0.74 | 1.8 |
| **20 Y** | 7.61 | **26.3** | 0.83 | 2.0 |
| **30 Y** | 7.26 | 25.2 | 0.98 | 2.4 |

**Not one maturity is redundant at the precision Treasury prints.** The best case, the
7 and 10 year on daily changes, still leaves **1.8 times** the rounding floor. The
worst, the 1 month on levels, leaves **35 times**.

**And the shape is itself a finding.** The middle of the curve is nearly interpolable
and the ends are not: on changes the 5 to 10 year sits at 1.8 to 2.1 times floor while
the 1 month sits at 10.3. **The shared procedure works in the middle and fails at the
ends**, which is where policy and supply bite.

## Part B: the registered column-addition test

Base set is the eleven always-present maturities. `k` is the factors needed to reach
99 per cent. Windows are chosen so each added column is absent before and present
after, with the other addition outside the window.

| addition | quantity | days before | days after | k before, base | k after, base | **k after, with new** | raised |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2 M, from 2018-10-16 | levels | 698 | 1002 | 2 | 3 | 3 | no |
| 2 M, from 2018-10-16 | **changes** | 698 | 1002 | 8 | **7** | **8** | **yes** |
| 4 M, from 2022-10-19 | levels | 1002 | 550 | 3 | 4 | 4 | no |
| 4 M, from 2022-10-19 | **changes** | 1002 | 550 | 7 | **6** | **7** | **yes** |

**On daily changes, adding a column raised the factor count by exactly one, both
times.** The direct test agrees: predicted from the other eleven, the 2 month leaves
**4.62 times floor** on changes and the 4 month leaves **5.55**.

**On levels neither addition raised k**, but levels `k` is 2 to 4 only because the
level factor swamps everything, which is failure mode 112 in its original form. **The
levels row is reported and is not used.**

**Verdict on §4.9's second question: the added columns carry information. They are not
interpolation.** Every new maturity Treasury began publishing added a dimension that
the existing eleven did not contain.

## So B30-17 returns a coherent negative on both of its registered questions

Chunk one: **more than six factors to reach 99 per cent on changes**, so discriminator
one fails on the carrier. Chunk two: **new columns raise the count**, so the quoting
grid is not a tautological expansion.

**The carrier chosen as the cleanest case for discriminator one fails discriminator
one, twice, on criteria fixed before the data was pulled.**

**And the expectation going in was the opposite.** §4.9 wrote that CMT is itself an
interpolation from a smaller input set, so low rank was **partly guaranteed by
construction**. It is not low rank at the published precision. **Either the input set
is larger than assumed, or the published precision is fine enough to resolve what the
interpolation adds, and the input set was never collected, so this is not resolved.**

## What this does to discriminator one, and it is a demotion

**Discriminator one is much weaker than §1e made it look, and the weakness is
structural rather than a matter of this carrier.**

**"Each party copies its neighbours" and "the quote vector is low-rank at published
precision" are different claims.** A market in which everyone copies a neighbour and
then adds an idiosyncratic adjustment produces a **full-rank** quote matrix, because
the adjustments are independent across parties. **Discriminator one sees nothing
there.** It can only detect compression so extreme that the idiosyncratic part falls
below the measurement floor.

**So its honest scope is narrow:**

> **Discriminator one can confirm extreme compression when it finds it. It cannot
> refute copying when it fails to find it. A high measured rank is consistent with
> universal copying plus per-party noise, and this arm has now produced exactly that
> pattern twice.**

**The framework's actual claim needs the other discriminators**, and §1e already names
them: change the procedure and hold reality fixed (two), project disagreement onto the
shared manifold's tangent space (four), check whether the quoting party can compute
what H_real requires (five), swap the formula (six). **Only discriminator one has been
run, and it is the one least able to carry the claim.**

## Limits

1. **The residuals in part A are split-sample; the factor counts in part B are in
   sample.** With 550 to 1002 days against 11 or 12 columns overfitting is not
   expected, **but the held-out guard that changed the answer in B30-16 chunk three
   has not been run here.** Same debt as chunk one, now twice recorded.
2. **The window pairs are not balanced in length** (698 against 1002, 1002 against
   550) and both straddle large rate moves. **The base-set `k` changes between windows
   on its own**, 8 to 7 and 7 to 6 on changes, which is why the comparison is `k after
   base` against `k after with new` and not before against after.
3. **Two additions only.** The 20-year reintroduction of 2020 is inside the panel as a
   continuously present column here and was not tested; the dates for the other
   changes were never checked against Treasury announcements, as §4.9 required.
4. **Nothing here reads why a maturity carries its own dimension.** Auction supply,
   policy expectations and segmented demand all predict end-of-curve idiosyncrasy and
   none of them is tested.

---

# B30-17, chunk three: the negative re-read. Five things that could have produced it, and the tool being weak is only the fifth

**Written 2026-08-30, prompted by the question "what else could have caused the
failure besides the tool being weak".** Chunks one and two jumped to
`discriminator one is weak` and registered a demotion on that basis. **That jump was
too fast.** Here are the candidates, ordered by how much of the negative each could
explain, with what would settle each.

## A. The construction does not match the discriminator, and this is the biggest one

**Discriminator one is stated for N parties quoting M numbers. CMT has N = 1.**
Treasury publishes one object. There is no cross-party structure in the panel at all.

§1e-2 already said the cross-party tests do not apply to this carrier, and §4.9
repeated it. **Then the arm was run anyway and the result was read as bearing on the
discriminator.** What was actually measured is **how compressible one publisher's
vector-valued time series is**, which is a different question from **whether many
parties share a procedure**.

**This alone may account for the whole negative**, and nothing in chunks one or two
separates it from the others.

## B. The floor is a lower bound on the noise, not the noise

The rounding grid assumes **the only error is rounding**. A published CMT rate also
carries the bid-ask of the input securities, the close-of-business snapshot timing,
and the interpolation's own approximation. **The true precision of the number is
coarser than 0.2887 basis points, possibly by a lot.**

```
if the real noise is about 2 bp rather than 0.29 bp
then 3 factors on daily changes  (1.26 bp)  are already BELOW it
and the verdict flips
```

**So every residual-over-floor ratio in chunks one and two is an upper bound.**

**What would settle it.** The honest floor is the disagreement between two independent
constructions of the same curve: Treasury CMT against the Fed's H.15, and against the
Gürkaynak, Sack and Wright zero-coupon curve. **Two people building "the same" curve
from the same market disagree by some amount, and that amount is the measurement
precision.** Neither series has been pulled.

## C. The criterion branch that fired is the unprincipled half of the registered pair

§4.9 registered two kinds of branch: **a floor-based one** and **a threshold on
variance share** ("more than six factors to reach 99 per cent means failure"). **The
branch that fired is the variance-share one**, and 99 per cent and six were both
picked by hand and derive from nothing.

**That is failure mode 112 inside the criteria themselves.** The principled branch
gives a different reading: on daily changes **k = 8 does fall below the floor**, so
the curve carries eight numbers out of eleven, which is partial compression rather
than none.

**Recorded as a criterion-design fault. The negative stands as registered, and it
should not have been registered in that form.**

## D. Part B's factor count is partly mechanical

Adding a twelfth column and asking again for 99 per cent of a now-larger total: **if
the new column is even slightly independent it contributes its own variance and needs
its own factor to reach the same share.** So "raised by exactly one, both times" is
close to the null prediction for a column that is mostly but not entirely predictable.

**The direct prediction residuals are the real content of part B** (2 month at 4.62
times floor, 4 month at 5.55 on changes), **and the factor-count table is demoted to
illustration.**

## E. The quantity may be the wrong one

Daily changes at the short end carry bill supply, repo specials and month-end
effects. Those are real, and they are not "the curve carrying more numbers" in the
sense the discriminator asks about. **Weekly or monthly changes might compress
further and were not run.**

## F. Only after all of the above does the tool being weak apply

The §1e-3 argument stands on its own logic: **copying plus independent per-party
adjustment produces a full-rank matrix, so a high rank cannot refute copying.** That
is true regardless of this carrier.

**But B30-17 is poor evidence for it**, because of A, and the demotion should not have
been hung on this arm. **§1e-3 is kept for its argument and its supporting citation to
this arm is withdrawn.**

## What this chunk changes

1. **B30-17's negative is not evidence that discriminator one is weak.** It is
   evidence that this carrier was the wrong shape for it, plus a criterion that
   contained a variance-share branch.
2. **The residual-over-floor numbers are upper bounds** until an independent
   construction of the curve is pulled.
3. **§1e-3 keeps its argument and loses its citation.**
4. **The arm is not closed.** It needs the independent-curve floor, and it needs to be
   re-read on the floor-based branch alone.

**And the general lesson, which is the one worth carrying:** a preregistered criterion
protects against choosing the verdict after seeing the data. **It does not protect
against the criterion being wrong.** Both faults here, the construction mismatch and
the variance-share branch, were written into the design file before any data was
pulled and were visible there.

---

---

# B30-16, chunk four: the first cross-party run. One shared dimension, and the rest is not shared

**Read 2026-08-30** against §1e-2's **test A**, which asks the question discriminator
one cannot: **a single shared manifold, or several manifolds that happen to sum to the
same dimension.** This is the first run of anything other than discriminator one.

**Carrier**: Table 6, 120 quarters, one row per (carrier, route) log-fare series
through time, demeaned in time. Series below 60 per cent quarter coverage dropped,
remaining gaps filled by time interpolation, **which is a stated approximation and not
data**. Seven carriers survive: AA 533 routes, DL 932, WN 623, UA 321, US 202, AS 89,
G4 43.

## The instrument, and why it needs no floor

Take each carrier's **own** top-three time-factor subspace and measure the **principal
angles** between carriers. **Principal angles need no noise floor and no variance
threshold**, which is why they are the headline here and the variance-share comparison
is secondary. That choice is failure mode 112 applied in advance for once.

## The reading

Each carrier's own top three factors explain 0.696 (UA) to 0.917 (WN) of its variance.

| principal angle | observed mean | observed median | observed max |
|---|---|---|---|
| **first** | **15.1°** | **11.7°** | 27.3° |
| second | 32.5° | 30.9° | 50.6° |
| third | 70.5° | 72.6° | 89.5° |

**Shuffled null, first angle: mean 39.0°, minimum 14.2° over 210 draws.**

**1. The leading direction is shared, and it is not close.** The observed median first
angle, 11.7°, is **below the null's minimum** across ten shuffles and twenty-one
carrier pairs. **Seven carriers' leading fare-time factor points nearly the same way.**

**2. Sharing degrades fast and is gone by the third factor.** Second angles sit at
30.9° median; third angles at 72.6°, close to orthogonal. **Beyond the first
dimension, carriers' time structures are their own.**

**3. The variance-share comparison sees none of this and would have said "shared".**
Pooled top-three share is **0.757** against a mean individual share of **0.776**.
Pooled is close to individual and nowhere near their sum, which is test A's stated
signature for a shared manifold. **The angles say that reading is wrong beyond the
first factor.** A variance share is blunter than a subspace angle, which is failure
mode 112 turning up in a third form.

## Verdict

> **Partial sharing. One shared dimension out of three. The seven carriers do not
> share a manifold; they share a direction.**

**WITHDRAWN 2026-08-30 by chunk five.** This verdict came from comparing the second
and third angles against the FIRST angle's null, which limit 1 below already flagged
as not a comparison. With the matched null, **two dimensions are shared and the
verdict reverses.** The paragraphs below are kept as the record of the error.

**That is the first evidence in this chain that bears on the copying claim rather than
on compressibility**, and it supports a weak form of it: there is a common component
that every carrier's fare path lies along, and there is carrier-specific structure
that is not common. **Neither H_taut nor H_real in their pure forms survives it.**

## Limits, and the first one blocks two thirds of the table

1. **The null was computed for the first angle only.** For a random pair of
   three-dimensional subspaces the three angles have different distributions, with the
   third much larger, **so comparing the observed 72.6° against a first-angle null of
   39° is not a comparison.** **The second and third rows cannot be read until the
   matched null is computed.** Only the first-angle result stands. **This is a fault in
   the run and it is recorded rather than worked around.**
2. **The coverage filter does most of the sample selection.** Seven carriers out of
   seventy-nine, and G4 enters on 43 routes. Whether the finding survives a lower
   threshold is unknown.
3. **Gap filling by time interpolation** puts smooth structure into series that had
   holes, which biases the leading factor toward agreement. **The direction of this
   bias is toward the finding, so the first-angle result is an upper bound on
   sharing.**
4. **Table 6 records a carrier only when it is the largest or the lowest-fare on a
   route**, so each carrier's series is an outcome-selected sample of its own pricing.
5. **A shared leading direction is what a common cost shock also produces.** Fuel,
   labour and the aggregate demand cycle would all put every carrier's fare path on
   one common component. **Nothing here separates a shared procedure from a shared
   environment**, and that separation is discriminator two's job, not this one's.

---

---

# B30-16, chunk five: the matched null, and it reverses chunk four

**Read 2026-08-30.** Chunk four flagged that its null covered only the first
principal angle and that two thirds of its table therefore could not be read. **The
matched null has now been computed for every angle, and it does not confirm chunk
four. It reverses it.**

Run at three coverage thresholds, which also settles chunk four's second limit.

| coverage | carriers | angle | **observed median** | null mean | null min | below null min |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0.4 | 12 | 1 | **11.78°** | 42.41° | 11.17° | no |
| 0.4 | 12 | 2 | **33.49°** | 76.73° | 43.03° | **yes** |
| 0.4 | 12 | 3 | 72.13° | 86.00° | 68.00° | no |
| 0.6 | 7 | 1 | **11.72°** | 39.03° | 14.15° | **yes** |
| 0.6 | 7 | 2 | **30.90°** | 78.35° | 51.83° | **yes** |
| 0.6 | 7 | 3 | 72.64° | 86.23° | 66.53° | no |
| 0.8 | 5 | 1 | **12.82°** | 27.62° | 19.14° | **yes** |
| 0.8 | 5 | 2 | **28.50°** | 78.37° | 63.46° | **yes** |
| 0.8 | 5 | 3 | 71.32° | 86.73° | 78.34° | **yes** |

Carrier sets: 0.4 gives AA AS B6 CO DL F9 FL G4 NW UA US WN; 0.6 drops five; 0.8
leaves AA AS DL UA WN.

## What changed, and why chunk four got it wrong

**Chunk four compared the observed second angle, 30.9°, against the FIRST angle's null
of 39° and concluded that sharing had degraded.** The second angle's own null is
**78.35°**. **So 30.9° is not a degraded second angle. It is a decisively shared one**,
sitting at less than half the null mean and below the null minimum at every threshold.

**The error was using one null for three quantities whose null distributions differ by
construction.** It was flagged in chunk four as a blocking limit and it turned out to
have inverted the verdict, not merely blurred it.

## The reading

**1. Two dimensions are shared, decisively.** Angle 1 at 11.7 to 12.8 degrees against
a null mean of 27.6 to 42.4; angle 2 at 28.5 to 33.5 against a null mean of 76.7 to
78.4. **Both are far below their own nulls at every coverage threshold.**

**2. The third is partially shared and close to the null.** 71.3 to 72.6 degrees
against a null mean of 86.0 to 86.7. Below, but by much less, and it crosses the
crude "below the null minimum" line only at the strictest threshold.

**3. The observed angles barely move across coverage thresholds**, 11.7 to 12.8, 28.5
to 33.5, 71.3 to 72.6, **while the carrier set goes from twelve to five.** Chunk four's
second limit said the 60 per cent filter might be doing the selection. **It is not.**

**4. The "below the null minimum" test is the weak part of this and its sensitivity
tracks sample size.** Ten shuffles give a crude minimum, and the null mean comparison
is where the margins are, which is why the verdict is stated on the means.

## Verdict, replacing chunk four's

> **The seven to twelve carriers share a two-dimensional manifold in fare-time space,
> not merely a direction. A third dimension is weakly shared. Chunk four's "one
> dimension, a direction not a manifold" is withdrawn.**

**This is the strongest evidence the chain has produced for the copying claim**, and it
is from the discriminator built for the question rather than from discriminator one.

## The caveat that chunk four already stated and that this does not remove

**A shared low-dimensional fare-time structure is exactly what a shared environment
also produces.** Fuel, labour, the aggregate demand cycle and a common regulatory
regime would put every carrier's fare path in one low-dimensional space without any
party copying any other. **Two shared dimensions rather than one makes the shared
environment reading MORE comfortable, not less**, since a small number of common
macro drivers is the obvious candidate.

**Separating a shared procedure from a shared environment is discriminator two's job:
change the procedure, hold reality fixed.** Nothing in B30-16 does that, and this
chunk does not claim otherwise.


## Superseded 2026-09-03 by chunk six, in this scope and no wider

**What does not survive.** The null was computed on the matrix **after** the
interpolation, so it does not carry the fill that the observed side carries. With
a fill-carrying null the cov 0.6 second angle reads 30.90 against a minimum of
20.70, so **the "below null min: yes" flag on the cov 0.6 second-angle row is
wrong**, and that is the row this block quotes in its own headline. The cov 0.8
and cov 0.95 rows survive the rebuilt null. All three null means fall.

**What does survive.** The reversal of chunk four's reading at the two stricter
thresholds; the finding that two directions are shared and a third weakly;
reading 3, once it is restated on positions rather than on raw angles.

**What is superseded outright.** The sentence "this is the strongest evidence the
chain has produced for the copying claim". The three shared directions are a
thirty-year drift, the 2008 episode and the 2020 shutdown, so this carrier
carries no information about the copying claim in either direction. The caveat
below anticipated this and could not settle it; chunk six settles it.

## Limits

1. **Ten shuffles.** The null minimum is crude; the null means are stable and the
   verdict rests on them.
2. **Gap filling by time interpolation** still biases toward agreement, as chunk four
   said. **The finding is an upper bound on sharing.**
3. **Table 6 selects on the outcome**, so each carrier's series is not a random sample
   of its own pricing.
4. **k was fixed at three throughout.** Whether the shared subspace is exactly
   two-dimensional or whether more dimensions would also be shared has not been tested.

# B30-16, chunk six: the null was computed after the fill, and once the null is removed the three shared directions can be named

**Read 2026-09-03.** Chunk five reversed chunk four by computing a matched null
for every principal angle. **The observed angles did not move. Only the null
did.** This block re-reads that same run twice: once to repair the null, and once
with the null removed and replaced by two measured anchors. Script
`experiments/b30_16_stage4_anchors.py`, records
`results/b30_16/stage4_anchors_cov60.json`, `..._cov80.json`, `..._cov95.json`.
No new data was collected.

## 1. The coverage census, which neither earlier block ran

| carrier | routes at cov >= 0.6 | >= 0.8 | >= 0.95 | **complete** | interpolated share of the cov 0.8 block |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| DL | 932 | 427 | 85 | **0** | 0.109 |
| WN | 623 | 345 | 143 | **0** | 0.084 |
| AA | 533 | 227 | 49 | **0** | 0.111 |
| UA | 321 | 115 | 6 | **0** | 0.125 |
| AS | 89 | 53 | 12 | **0** | 0.091 |
| US | 202 | 0 | 0 | **0** | n/a |
| G4 | 43 | 0 | 0 | **0** | n/a |

**Not one of the 10,021 route series is observed in all 120 quarters, on any of
the seventy-nine carriers.** Every block in stage four is between 8 and 13 per
cent filled, and the two carriers that only the loosest threshold admits, US and
G4, contribute **zero** surviving routes at cov 0.8.

## 2. The null was computed after the fill

`rng.permutation(M[:, t])` shuffles rows within each column of a block that has
**already been interpolated and demeaned**. That preserves each carrier's own
quarterly cross-sectional mean exactly and destroys everything else, so under it
each carrier's subspace is its own aggregate trend plus two noise directions,
which is why its second and third angles sit near 78 and 86 degrees. **The
observed subspaces carry the interpolation. The null subspaces do not.**

Rebuilt so that it does: shuffle the observed values within each column, keep the
missing-value mask, then interpolate and demean by the identical path.

| cov | angle | observed median | null as run, mean / min | fill-carrying null, mean / min |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0.6 | 1 | 11.72 | 39.03 / 14.15 | 25.83 / 12.64 |
| 0.6 | **2** | **30.90** | 78.35 / **51.83** | **57.12 / 20.70** |
| 0.8 | 1 | 12.82 | 27.62 / 19.14 | 26.56 / 18.38 |
| 0.8 | 2 | 28.50 | 78.37 / 63.46 | 66.02 / 43.34 |
| 0.95 | 1 | 14.40 | 37.49 / 28.18 | 37.62 / 29.01 |
| 0.95 | 2 | 38.65 | 80.39 / 69.02 | 78.89 / 69.85 |

**Chunk five's "below the null minimum" flag on the second angle at cov 0.6 does
not survive**: 30.90 sits above a fill-carrying minimum of 20.70. **That is the
row chunk five quotes in its own headline.** At cov 0.8 and 0.95 the second angle
clears the rebuilt minimum, so the reversal of chunk four holds at the two
stricter thresholds and not at the loose one.

**The internal check is the cov 0.95 row.** At 3.5 per cent fill the two nulls
agree to within 1.5 degrees on every angle, which is what a fill-carrying null
must do where there is nothing to fill. It does.

## 3. Repairing the null is not the fix. The line is the wrong shape

"Below the minimum of ten shuffles" is a threshold anchored on what one run
happened to produce. This project has already measured what that construction is
worth: the registered tables built on such lines stopped nothing, and every
reading that returned something was an object printed. **A tenth-order statistic
used as a hard line is the same construction under another name.** The rest of
this block therefore carries no null at all.

## 4. Two anchors, and the reading becomes a position rather than a verdict

**Ceiling.** Two random halves of **one** carrier's own routes. Same pricing
procedure by construction, same quarters, same missingness pattern, same fill.
This is what the instrument reads when sharing is total. Five splits per carrier.

**Floor.** The same blocks with the route-to-time association destroyed and the
mask and the fill preserved, which is section 2's rebuilt null used as a
measurement instead of as a test. This is what the fill alone delivers.

| cov | angle | ceiling | observed | floor | position, floor to ceiling |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0.6 | 1 | 3.95 | 11.72 | 25.93 | 64.7 % |
| 0.6 | 2 | 10.16 | 30.90 | 63.65 | 61.2 % |
| 0.6 | 3 | 25.43 | 72.64 | 84.96 | 20.7 % |
| 0.8 | 1 | 4.37 | 12.82 | 25.50 | 60.0 % |
| 0.8 | 2 | 10.88 | 28.50 | 65.95 | 68.0 % |
| 0.8 | 3 | 21.41 | 71.32 | 85.86 | 22.6 % |
| 0.95 | 1 | 4.03 | 14.40 | 37.17 | 68.7 % |
| 0.95 | 2 | 15.88 | 38.65 | 79.78 | 64.4 % |
| 0.95 | 3 | 37.08 | 72.07 | 87.51 | 30.6 % |

**The first two angles sit at the same place on the scale, around two thirds of
the way up, at every threshold**, while the carrier count goes seven to five to
three and the fill goes from 11 per cent to 3.5 per cent. **The third sits at a
fifth to a third and is separated from the other two everywhere.**

**The raw angle moves and the position does not, and that is the whole case for
having a floor.** The second angle reads 30.90 at cov 0.6 and 38.65 at cov 0.95,
which taken alone says sharing weakens as the fill is stripped out. Its floor
moves with it, 63.65 to 79.78, and the position holds at 61 and 64 per cent.
**The fill was moving the reading and the benchmark together and in the same
direction, which is precisely what a benchmark not carrying the fill cannot
see.**

## 5. How many dimensions are shared, on a scale nothing in this run supplies

Average the projectors, `P = (1/C) sum_c V_c V_c^T`. Its eigenvalues lie in
`[0, 1]` by construction: one means the direction lies in every carrier's
subspace, `1/C` means it lies in exactly one, and the top `k` sum to `k`. **The
endpoints come from the algebra and not from a shuffle.**

| cov | `1/C` | observed | floor | ceiling, median over carriers | position |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0.6 | 0.143 | 0.958, 0.803, 0.445 | 0.881, 0.608, 0.410 | 0.996, 0.959, 0.693 | 67 %, 56 %, **12 %** |
| 0.8 | 0.200 | 0.977, 0.808, 0.585 | 0.909, 0.525, 0.298 | 0.995, 0.959, 0.828 | 78 %, 65 %, 54 % |
| 0.95 | 0.333 | 0.979, 0.854, 0.612 | 0.855, 0.486, 0.389 | 0.999, 0.981, 0.930 | 86 %, 74 %, 41 % |

**Two directions sit high and a third does not**, which is chunk five's verdict
reached with no null anywhere in it. **The third direction at cov 0.6 sits at 12
per cent, which is at the floor**, and cov 0.6 is where the fill is heaviest.

## 6. Out of sample: the shared subspace applied to a carrier it never saw

Build the shared subspace from every carrier **except one**, then read how much
of the held-out carrier's own route variation it captures. The subspace has never
seen those routes, so this is a prediction and not a fit, and **it needs no null
of any kind**: a `k`-dimensional subspace drawn at random captures `k/T` by
construction, the carrier's own top `k` captures the most any `k` dimensions can,
and those two bracket the reading.

**The floor here is the same construction as section 4's, and it is worth naming
precisely.** The within-column shuffle preserves each carrier's own quarterly
cross-sectional mean exactly, so the floor subspace is not empty: it is **that
carrier's own aggregate fare trend plus the geometry the missingness and the fill
put in**. The floor therefore grants the alternative everything except
route-level co-movement, which is the one thing being claimed.

| cov | own, in sample | **shared, out of sample** | **fill and own trend** | random `k` dims | shared minus floor |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0.6 | 0.7554 | **0.6498** | **0.6548** | 0.0203 | **-0.005** |
| 0.8 | 0.7390 | **0.6649** | **0.5834** | 0.0335 | **+0.081** |
| 0.95 | 0.7301 | **0.6296** | **0.4744** | 0.0358 | **+0.155** |

Medians over the held-out carriers. Per carrier at cov 0.95: AA 0.5503 against
0.3758, DL 0.6296 against 0.4744, WN 0.8639 against 0.6059.

**1. The shared subspace predicts a carrier it never saw, and it recovers 86 to
90 per cent of what that carrier's own factors capture in sample.** Against a
random three dimensions at 0.02 to 0.06 this is not close, and no distributional
assumption enters anywhere.

**2. At cov 0.6 the shared subspace does not beat the floor.** Median -0.005, and
across the seven held-out carriers it wins four, loses two and ties one, every
margin under 0.03 except G4. **Everything the shared subspace does at the loose
threshold is done as well by aggregate trends and fill geometry alone.**

**3. The advantage grows monotonically as the fill is stripped out**, from -0.005
to +0.081 to +0.155 while the fill goes from its heaviest to 3.5 per cent.
**That is the direction a real shared component must move in and the opposite of
the direction an interpolation artifact moves in.** It is also the sharpest
statement available about which of chunk five's three thresholds is worth
quoting: the strictest, not the loosest.

**4. This settles that the structure is real.** Section 4 establishes that the
observed reading sits well above what the fill delivers; this section establishes
that the same structure predicts data it was not built from, which the position
on a scale cannot show. **The two answer different questions and both are
needed.**

**What it does not do is identify the structure**, and section 7 does that.

---

## 7. The directions, printed

A principal angle is a number about geometry. The shared directions are quarterly
series, and printing their extreme quarters costs nothing.

| direction | eigenvalue | highest quarters | lowest quarters | what it is |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 0.958 to 0.979 | 1996Q2 to 1997Q3 | 2022Q2 to 2025Q4 | **a monotone drift across the whole window** |
| 2 | 0.803 to 0.854 | **2008Q1 to 2010Q1** | 1996 to 1997 at cov 0.95; 2022 to 2024 at cov 0.6 and 0.8 | **the 2008 fuel and credit episode and its 2009 to 2011 aftermath** |
| 3 | 0.445 to 0.612 | **2020Q2 to 2021Q1** at cov 0.8 and 0.95 | 2022Q2 to 2023Q1 | **the 2020 shutdown** |

**All three are dated common shocks and every one of them arrived from outside
the industry.** Direction one is a thirty-year drift. Direction two peaks in
2008Q1 to 2010Q1 at all three thresholds. Direction three peaks in 2020Q2 to
2021Q1 and is the **least** shared of the three, which settles what the
eigenvalue measures: **not how exogenous a shock was, but how uniformly it loaded
across route networks.** The shutdown was the most exogenous event in the window
and the least uniform in its incidence.

## 8. Standing

> **The shared subspace is spanned by dated common shocks. Any set of carriers
> facing the same shocks under any pricing rule monotone in demand produces this
> reading, so the reading carries no information about whether the procedure is
> shared. The carrier does not contain the object the question asks about.**

**This refutes nothing and supports nothing.** It is the third state, and it is
the disposition [`b9_zero_holonomy.md`](b9_zero_holonomy.md) §27.3 reached on its
own carrier: the test is sound, and the carrier lacks the object it needs.
**Chunk five's sentence "this is the strongest evidence the chain has produced
for the copying claim" is superseded.** Chunk five stated the competing reading
as a caveat it could not remove. Printing the directions converts that caveat
into an identification, which is a stronger statement than the caveat and one
that closes the block.

**Separating a shared procedure from a shared environment is discriminator two's
job, and B30-18 is where the chain does it**: change the procedure, hold reality
fixed. Nothing in B30-16 does that, and chunk four and chunk five both said so.

## 9. What chunks four and five keep

1. **The structure is real and low dimensional, and section 6 is the reason to
   say so rather than section 4.** Two directions sit 60 to 78 per cent of the
   way from the fill floor to the same-carrier ceiling on both scales and at
   every threshold, and the subspace built without a carrier predicts that
   carrier at 86 to 90 per cent of its own in-sample ceiling.
2. **Chunk four's observation stands and was never in dispute**: alignment
   degrades monotonically, 11.7 to 30.9 to 72.6. What chunk four got wrong was
   inferring "not shared" at the second angle from that degradation. **Chunk
   five's `WITHDRAWN` label is wider than the correction it made**, and section 4
   puts a magnitude on the degradation that neither block had: 65, 61 and 21 per
   cent of the way up.
3. **The coverage filter is not doing the selection.** Chunk five claimed this on
   the raw angles, where the second angle in fact moves by a quarter. The
   positions establish it properly: seven carriers to three, and the position
   moves by four points.

## Limits

1. **The ceiling splits routes, not quarters.** Two halves of one carrier's
   routes share a procedure and also share that carrier's own network, so the
   ceiling is an upper anchor for both together.
2. **Five splits per carrier.** Spread across splits on the second angle is 2.4
   to 6.4 degrees, except AS at 12.1 with 44 routes per half.
3. **`k` fixed at three**, as in chunks four and five.
4. **The fill is still on the observed side and no threshold removes it**, since
   no series is complete. The floor measures what the fill delivers. It does not
   take it out.
5. **The out-of-sample reading is leave-one-carrier-out, not leave-quarters-out.**
   A subspace lives in quarter space, so holding out quarters would change the
   ambient space rather than the sample. Holding out a carrier holds out routes,
   which is the sample that matters here, and it leaves the time dimension whole.
6. **The directions are named by their dated extremes, not by regression on a
   named macro series.** Fitting them against jet fuel, capacity and the cycle is
   one fetch away and was not done here.
