# B30-23, chunk eight: the pre-transfer rating plan, read, and the first counts

**Read 2026-09-03.** The acquired book's last accepted rating plan before the
transfer was retrieved and parsed. **1,415 pages, 184 distinct page headers.**
This is the first block in this arm that contains numbers rather than navigation.

## The renaming is now confirmed from inside the document

Chunk five inferred from an address that the interface shows current names against
historical filings. **The document settles it: every page of a January 2021
filing is headed with the acquired group's own company name**, while the search
interface displays the acquirer's name for the same NAIC code. **The inference was
right and it no longer rests on an address.**

## The same-table condition holds, on five of the six

Chunk seven flagged that the criterion compares the same table on both sides, that
the before side is one document and the after side is six named tables, and that
this was unchecked and could void the pair. **It holds.** Page counts, before
against after:

| partition | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| classification factors | **470** | 450 |
| personal credit factors | 41 | 40 |
| household composition #1 | **20** | **20** |
| prior insurance tier factors | **19** | **19** |
| a mobile-enrolment factor | 9 | one rule page |

**Five partitions match by name and nearly by extent.** The pair supports the
criterion.

## The counts, pre-transfer side

**Every table is one row per cell with a single factor column**, checked by eye on
two of them before parsing.

| table | pages | **cells** | **distinct values** | collisions | share |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| prior insurance tier | 19 | 1,386 | **353** | 1,033 | 74.5 % |
| household composition #1 | 20 | 750 | **102** | 648 | 86.4 % |
| personal credit | 41 | 3,312 | **827** | 2,485 | 75.0 % |
| **classification** | 470 | **22,032** | **3,274** | 18,758 | **85.1 %** |

> **Twenty-two thousand cells produce three thousand values.**

**That is the counting law's own claim, at scale, on a live commercial tariff that
was in force.** The claim is that what a procedure produces is the number of
distinct class values it writes and not the number of blocks it draws, and the two
differ here by a factor of seven. **The identity factor alone occupies 3,792 of
the classification cells, seventeen per cent of the table**, which are blocks
drawn that do nothing at all.

## One class line, two blocks, read on its own

The classification table's own column headers name its partitions, and one of them
has exactly two blocks. **Nine thousand four hundred and fifty cells carry a
complete pair.**

| | |
|---|---|
| pairs whose two values differ | 8,400, 88.89 % |
| **pairs whose two values are exactly equal** | **1,050, 11.11 %** |
| distinct values written on the first block | 2,208 |
| distinct values written on the second | 2,396 |
| **union** | **3,225, not 4,604** |
| **values written on both blocks** | **1,379** |

**A two-block line does not double the value count.** It adds 1,017 rather than
2,396, because 1,379 of its values are shared with the other block. **And on 1,050
cells the line is written and separates nothing**, which is the empty-block case
at cell resolution rather than at the level of a whole class.

**The equal share is 1,050 of 9,450, which is one ninth exactly.** That is too
clean to be an accident and suggests the line is switched off on a ninth of the
design by construction. **Unexplained and recorded, not interpreted.**

**And the direction is not one-sided**: on the differing pairs the ratio between
the two blocks runs from 0.8677 to 1.5273 with a median of 1.1122, **so one block
is not uniformly dearer than the other.**

## What this block does not do

**It has one side.** The counts above are the pre-transfer plan alone. **The
criterion is a comparison and the post-transfer plan has not been parsed**, so no
verdict is available and none is offered.

## Limits

1. **One side, four tables.** The other 180 page headers were not parsed.
2. **The parse takes the last decimal token on a line as the value** and was
   checked against the printed layout on two tables. A table with more than one
   factor column would be miscounted, and 180 were not inspected.
3. **The one-ninth regularity is a pattern in a count**, with no mechanism read
   from the document.

---

---

# B30-23, chunk nine: the pair was two revisions of one program, and the document said so in its first sentence

**Read 2026-09-03.** Chunks seven and eight built toward scoring the criterion on
a pair of accepted filings. **A score was written and withdrawn before it was
entered.** This block records why, and what the pair does and does not support.

## What the filing says it is

The post-transfer filing carries a one-page introduction, in the same archive as
the rate pages. Its first sentence:

> a rate revision to a **new program which was approved by the department on
> September 20, 2018**, with that program's own tracking number cited

and its second names its immediate predecessor as **the January 2021 filing**,
which is the one chunk seven took as the pre-transfer side.

> **So the two are consecutive revisions of one program, and the program belongs
> to the acquired party.**

**Consecutive revisions of one program have the same partition by construction.**
The finding that every one of 22,032 keys matched, and that only a subset of
values moved, is what that pair had to produce under any hypothesis. **It carries
nothing about whether a procedure travelled.**

**The subset that moved has a plain reading too**, given in the same introduction:
the class table was revised for drivers under 27. **That is the age-band pattern,
and it is an ordinary actuarial revision.** The rate change is itemised at 3.5,
2.1, 0.2, 0.2 and 3.5 per cent by component for 9.8 overall, supported by
countrywide experience.

## The error, named

**The side key was taken from the filing prefix**, which changed from the acquired
group's filing organisation to the acquirer's on the same entity. **A prefix says
who submitted a document. It does not say whose procedure is inside it.** The
acquirer's filing organisation submitted a revision to the acquired party's
program: the prefix moved and the program did not.

**That is the third identifier on this carrier that looks like it carries
provenance and does not**, after the displayed company name and the displayed
address. **Provenance is carried by what the document says about itself, and
nothing else here carries it.**

## What can be said, and its source is a memorandum rather than a count

**At the first accepted post-transfer filing the book was still on its own
program, and the party had tried to move it and been refused.** Two filings a
month earlier under the acquirer's prefix carried its manuals as replacements for
this book and the state rejected them.

**That is a reading about a date, not a measurement of a count**, and it is
recorded at that strength. **The criterion has not been scored on this carrier.**

## What the counts in chunk eight are still worth

**Chunk eight took only the pre-transfer side and offered no verdict**, and it
stands unchanged. Its numbers are a reading of the counting law on a live tariff
that was in force: **22,032 blocks producing 3,274 values**, and one two-block
class line writing 2,208 and 2,396 values that union to 3,225 rather than 4,604.
**Those do not depend on the pair being a before and after of anything.**

## What the next retrieval has to be

**A filing whose own introduction says the book is moving to a different program**,
rather than one revising the incumbent. **That is readable from the first sentence
and from the cited program number**, at no cost, before anything is parsed.

## Limits

1. **The rejected pair was not opened**, so whether they were refused on substance
   or on form is unread.
2. **No later filing has been searched for.** Whether an accepted migration exists
   after March 2022 is unknown.
3. **The score that was written has been withdrawn and kept**, and is not in the
   ledger.

---

---

# B30-23, chunk ten: the terminal state, and the criterion turns out to be the wrong shape

**Read 2026-09-03.** Chunk nine left the arm unscored and named the next
retrieval. **Instead of walking forward year by year, the newest filing was read
first**, on the reasoning that a terminal observation implies every intermediate
one. **It does, and it settles the arm.**

## The terminal observation

| | |
|---|---|
| tracking | `FAIG-134773153` |
| submitted / disposed | 18 December 2025 / **25 February 2026**, accepted |
| entity | NAIC **26298**, the same one throughout |
| **memorandum** | names the acquired party's own program |
| **actuarial support** | the same program name again, twice |
| **the table** | **classification factors, still 450 pages, replaced** |

> **Four years and ten months after the change of control, the acquired book is
> still priced by its own program, and the table this arm counted is still the
> same table.**

**One observation replaced four years of them.** Checking 2022, 2023, 2024 and
2025 separately would have returned the same answer four more times.

## The score, and its source is not a count

**The design file's second outcome holds: the tariff survived the change of
owner.** But the evidence is not the one chunk nine withdrew. That one compared
two consecutive revisions of a single program, which matches by construction.

**This one is program identity.** The acquired party's program is named in its own
filing memoranda in 2021, in 2022 and in 2025, and it is still the program in
force. **Meanwhile the acquiring party amended its own manuals under a memorandum
naming the migration, and that filing was accepted in February 2022.** Two
programs, both live, both filed by the same organisation, for five years.

**Reading five file names answered what 22,032 counted cells could not.** That is
the second time in one day that printing or reading the object beat counting it,
after the same thing happened on the fare-factor arm. **The counts from chunk eight
keep their value as a reading of the counting law on an in-force tariff, and they
were never needed for this question.**

## The criterion is the wrong shape, and this case is what showed it

**The criterion is binary: the count moves to the acquirer's, or it stays at its
own.** The observed structure is neither. **Both programs continue in parallel**,
one carrying the acquired book and one amended to carry something, for five years
and counting. **A binary question cannot report that**, and reporting it as "did
not travel" loses the fact that the acquirer did act, on its own manuals, on a
date, in an accepted filing.

**The realistic outcomes are at least four**: both programs persist; the book moves
to the acquirer's; the acquirer's is amended to receive the book's structures; new
business goes one way while renewals go the other. **The criterion admits two.**

## The carrier screen this case yields, and it costs nothing

**The question this arm needs is whether a migration happened, and a migration has
a signature on the filing index itself: the acquired program's filings stop.**

> **Screen every candidate transfer by asking whether the acquired entity kept
> filing its own program afterwards. If it did, there was no migration and no
> pair. If the filings cease, that is where the pair is.**

**That reads off a result list.** No memoranda, no downloads, no counts, and it
runs before a case is chosen rather than after five retrievals. **Registered as
the first screen for the next candidate**, ahead of the three pre-checks, because
it is cheaper than all of them.

## Still unread, and it is one document

**The memorandum attached to the accepted February 2022 filing** names both
parties and the direction and would say what that filing actually moved. **Whether
the acquirer's amended manuals took any of this book, or were prepared for
something else, is not established here.** The parallel structure above is read
off program names and dates, and that document would say more.

## Limits

1. **One state, one entity, one case.**
2. **The score rests on program identity across filings, not on a count.** It is
   the weaker of the two evidence types the design contemplated and it is the one
   that was available.
3. **The February 2022 filing's scope is unread**, and it is the only thing that
   could turn the parallel reading into something else.

---

---

# B30-23, chunk eleven: the memorandum was a name change, and that settles the arm

**Read 2026-09-03.** Chunks six, seven, nine and ten all treated one accepted
filing as an attempted or partial migration, on the strength of a memorandum whose
file name pairs the two parties and a date. **The memorandum has now been read.
It is a name change and it says so.**

## What it says

The acquiring group acquired **eight insuring entities** on the closing date. The
filing is submitted **to advise the department of administrative changes**, and it
states in its own second sentence that **the changes will not result in any change
to revision dates**. Four of the eight entities are renamed, from the acquired
group's brand to the acquirer's, and **once approvals complete the new names
replace the prior names on all filed rates and rules**, with the acquired group's
abbreviations changing to the acquirer's wherever they appear.

> **The filing changes the names on the documents and nothing else, and it says so
> in writing.**

## What that corrects

**The manuals attached to it are not the acquirer's legacy manuals.** They are the
acquired group's own manuals with the name swapped: the versions marked current
from 2019 are the acquired entities' 2019 manuals, and the proposed 2021 versions
are the same manuals renamed. **The two rejected filings a month earlier were the
same thing, refused and refiled.**

**So there is no migration anywhere in this record**, attempted or otherwise.
Chunk ten's reading of two programs running in parallel is wrong in its second
half: there is one program, the acquired party's, and a rename applied to it.

**The Rhode Island addresses are also explained.** Every entity in these filings is
one of the eight acquired ones, at their own headquarters, renamed. Chunk five
inferred a consolidation of operations from that address and there is no need for
it.

## The score, now correctly sourced

**Control moved and was exercised.** The acquirer's filing organisation submits
substantive rate changes for the entity, including a 9.8 per cent revision in 2022
and a classification revision in 2025. **It is not a passive holding.**

**And the acquirer's documented action on the pricing function was to change the
names on the documents while stating that nothing else changes.** Four years and
ten months later the acquired program is still in force with its partition intact.

> **The procedure did not travel. The party held the authority, used it on other
> things, and put in writing that the change was administrative.**

**That is the design file's second outcome, and this time the evidence is the
party's own statement rather than a count comparison.** `PROCEDURE-DID-NOT-TRAVEL
2026-09-03`.

## One pre-check was written wrong and is corrected here

Section 3a's third indicator reads **"a filing exists that attempts to move the
book onto the acquirer's own manuals"**, offered as the load-bearing sign that
control reached the pricing function. **On this carrier no such filing exists**,
and the one taken for it is a rename. **By that indicator this carrier would be
void rather than negative, and that would be wrong**, because the acquirer plainly
does set this tariff: it files the rate changes.

**The indicator is replaced**: **the acquirer's own filing organisation submits
substantive rate revisions for the acquired entity.** That is what shows the
pricing function changed hands, it holds here, and it does not presuppose the
answer the arm is trying to read.

## What this case is worth

**A clean negative on one carrier**, and three things that outlive it: **the screen
that finds the next one** (chunk ten: look for an acquired program whose filings
stop), **the correction above**, and **a fourth identifier that looks like it
carries provenance and does not** — after the displayed name, the displayed
address and the filing prefix, now **the file name of an attachment**. **A
document named for two parties and a direction was a name change.**

## Limits

1. **One state, one entity, one transfer.**
2. **Eight entities were acquired and four renamed here.** What happened to the
   other four is unread.
3. **The negative is about this acquirer's choice**, and a carrier where the
   acquirer did consolidate would answer a different and more informative
   question. **The screen in chunk ten is how to find one.**

---

---

# B30-23, chunk twelve: direction is not the question, and a candidate where one program is declared to absorb the other

**Read 2026-09-03.** Two things: a simplification of the criterion, and the next
carrier.

## Direction is not part of the claim

The arm was written as though the acquirer imposes its procedure on the target,
and chunk eleven's carrier was read for whether that happened. **It is the wrong
frame.** The claim is that a procedure propagated from one party to another.
**Which party's survived is decided by shareholder politics, by the negotiation
and by who holds sovereignty afterwards**, and the best-known industrial case runs
against ownership entirely: a buyer acquires a target and the target's management
and its procedure end up running the buyer.

**That is a fact about the merger and not about whether procedures travel.** **The named case is Boeing and McDonnell Douglas, 1997**: the buyer acquired the target and the target's executives and its cost-first procedure ended up running the buyer. **Which procedure survives a merger is a question of politics, of the negotiation and of sovereignty afterwards, and it sits outside anything a count can reach.**

**Dropping direction removes a condition and enlarges the pool.** Every case where
the smaller or the acquired side's procedure won was previously outside the arm's
frame, and those are not rare.

## And it collapses the pre-check to one observable

Section 3a listed three indicators for whether the treatment reached the pricing
function. **The first two hold on a carrier where nothing happened** — chunk
eleven's, where four entities were renamed and the party wrote that nothing else
changed — **and the third was the thing that was misread there.**

> **One observable replaces all three: does one of the two programs stop being
> filed?**

**A program still being revised is still in force. A program whose filings cease
has been replaced.** That reads off a result list before a case is chosen, which
is where a screen belongs.

## The candidate

**An acquirer's own public acquisition presentation states the plan in advance**,
which is the opposite of the previous carrier where the direction had to be dug
out of an attachment and turned out to be a rename:

- **one of the acquirer's own personal lines brands will be merged into the
  acquired company**
- the acquired company's **policy and claims platforms** are named as what enables
  that integration
- the acquired company's technology **will be used across the combined entity**

**So the absorbing program belongs to the acquired side and the absorbed book
belongs to the acquirer**, which under the paragraph above is neither here nor
there, **and what matters is that one program is declared to absorb another, in a
dated public document, before the fact.**

**Both outcomes are reachable on it**, which the previous carrier never had: if the
absorbed book ends up priced by the other program, its value count becomes that
program's, and that is the design's first outcome rather than its second.

## What has to be run, and it is the screen

> **Do the absorbed brand's own personal auto rate filings stop after the closing?**

Any open state, personal auto, that company name, **read the highest tracking
number and its year.** Stopping in the year of the closing or the one after is the
signature. **If they continue, this carrier is the previous one again and the next
candidate is drawn.**

## Limits

1. **Only the intent document has been read.** Whether the integration happened as
   declared is unchecked, and declarations of this kind are often partly
   abandoned.
2. **The screen has not been run.** No filing index has been searched for the
   absorbed brand.
3. **One acquirer, one year.** The pool this simplification opens has not been
   enumerated.

---

---

# B30-23, chunk thirteen: the second carrier dies on one lookup, and the two failures have one structural explanation

**Read 2026-09-03.** Chunk twelve named a candidate on the strength of an
acquirer's own presentation declaring that one of its personal lines brands would
be merged into the acquired company. **The screen was run and the candidate is
dead.**

## The screen, run once

| | |
|---|---|
| tracking | a filing accepted **14 April 2026** |
| entity | the brand that was declared to be merged in, still its own NAIC company |
| type | Rate/Rule, personal auto, its own factor and symbol pages |

> **Five years after the acquirer declared the brand would be merged into the
> acquired company, that brand is still filing its own personal auto rate pages.**

**The screen returns "the filings did not stop", so there was no migration and
there is no pair.** One lookup. **The previous carrier cost five retrievals, a
parse of 1,415 pages and a verdict that had to be withdrawn**, and the screen
would have killed it in the same one lookup. That is what chunk ten's screen was
for and it works.

**A declaration of intent is not evidence of migration**, and this is the instance
that shows it: the plan was stated in a dated public document by the party and
five years later the filings say it did not happen in the record.

## Two carriers, two negatives, and the explanation is structural

**A filed rating plan is observable because it is lodged with a regulator, per
entity.** And being lodged per entity is exactly why it survives a change of
owner: **moving a book to another entity's plan costs policyholder notice, policy
rewrites and fresh approval, while keeping the plan and changing the name on it
costs almost nothing.** The first carrier's own memorandum said as much in
writing, calling the change administrative and stating that revision dates would
not move.

> **Observability and mobility trade against each other. A procedure is easy to
> see here precisely because it is bolted to an entity, and being bolted to an
> entity is what stops it travelling.**

**So the most visible procedures are the least likely to propagate**, and two
negatives on this channel are not bad luck.

## What that says about where to look, stated as a selection rule

**Pick channels where keeping the incumbent procedure is not an option.** Ranked
by whether the old procedure can survive at all:

| channel | can the incumbent procedure stay | reading |
|---|---|---|
| **purchase of a failed firm's business** | **the entity is gone, so the procedure has no host** | **strongest**, but not in this industry, where a liquidation sends policies to a guaranty association rather than onto another plan. Wanted: an industry where the operating entity itself is sold and continues |
| **conditional funding** | **the condition is the instruction to change the procedure; keeping it is what forfeits the money** | strong, and the conditionality is published in dated letters |
| **cross-border investment** | only where the home procedure is lawful in the destination. **Where it is not, it cannot stay** | strong, and the constraint is set by statute rather than by negotiation |
| ordinary acquisition | **it can stay, and staying is the cheapest option** | **measured twice, stayed twice** |

**And the same rule points at a different kind of procedure entirely: published,
but not bolted to an entity.** Platform terms are the clean case. They are
published, they are filed with no regulator, they can be replaced wholesale on a
date, **and one of them was**, which is the positive instance sitting on the other
side of the same structural test.

## And it changes the question this arm should ask

**Hunting for the single case where a migration happened is the wrong search.**
The screen is cheap enough to run many times, and **the rate is the reading**:

> **Across N transfers in a line of business, how many end with the acquired
> program's filings stopping?**

**Zero out of N is a result, not a failure to find a carrier.** It says procedures
do not propagate through ownership in this line, they persist with the entity.
**That converts "no carrier found" from a blocker into an answer**, and it needs no
successful migration to produce one.

## Limits

1. **Two transfers, one line of business, one state each.** A rate is not
   available from two.
2. **The screen reads the filing record only.** A book can move economically
   through reinsurance or renewal rights without the filed plan changing, and
   this screen would not see it.
3. **The selection rule above is reasoning, not a measurement.** Nothing in the
   other three channels has been run.

---

---

# B30-18, chunk one: the procedure changed, 120 trillion was revalued, and the market did not move

**Read 2026-08-30** against §4.10's criterion B, fixed in the design file and repeated
in the script before any data was pulled. **This is the first run of discriminator
two.** Data: FRED SOFR and EFFR daily, 2,099 overlapping days.

## The operator, stated by the party

LCH, in its filing to the CFTC's MRAC, 2020-07-21:

> *"All USD discounted future cash flows (as calibrated under the Fed Funds
> discounting regime) are **held constant** and the compensation amount is calculated
> as the **differential between the present value of these cash flows**"*

**Cash flows held constant, procedure changed, money moves.** Auction 2020-10-16,
implementation 2020-10-19, completion announced 2020-10-20. **120 trillion dollars of
notional, over one million contracts, 24 billion net notional of compensating basis
swaps auctioned.**

**Rule 8a: all of the above was seen before the criteria were written.** That
compensation was paid is confirmatory and is not this arm's output.

## Criterion B: did the underlying market move

Basis is SOFR minus EFFR in basis points, published on a one basis point grid.
Pre-window robust sigma of daily changes: **1.483 bp**.

**Event window, no day exceeds 0.67 sigma:**

| date | basis | change | sigma |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2020-10-15 | 1.00 | +1.00 | +0.67 |
| **2020-10-16** | 0.00 | −1.00 | −0.67 |
| **2020-10-19** | 0.00 | +0.00 | 0.00 |
| 2020-10-20 | −1.00 | −1.00 | −0.67 |
| 2020-10-21 | −2.00 | −1.00 | −0.67 |

**Level, at every window length:**

| window | pre mean | post mean | **shift** | sigma | post ends |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| ±5 d | −0.200 | −0.800 | **−0.600 bp** | −0.40 | 2020-10-29 |
| ±10 d | −0.100 | 0.000 | **+0.100 bp** | +0.07 | 2020-11-05 |
| ±20 d | −0.550 | 0.100 | +0.650 bp | +0.44 | 2020-11-20 |
| ±40 d | −0.500 | −0.375 | +0.125 bp | +0.08 | 2020-12-21 |
| ±60 d | −0.417 | −0.433 | **−0.017 bp** | −0.01 | 2021-01-21 |

**The traded spread between the two curves did not move.** Every window from five days
to sixty gives a shift inside half a sigma, and the ±5 day window, which ends before
the election, gives the same answer as the ±60 which contains it. **The confound the
design file required be excluded does not drive this.**

## The reading

> **A clearing house changed which curve it discounts at. 120 trillion of notional and
> over a million contracts were revalued and cash had to move between members. The
> market price of the relationship between those two curves moved by less than half a
> basis point.**

**That is discriminator two's operator running in a real market on a dated event: the
procedure moved, the valuations moved with it, and the world did not.**

## How quiet was that week, actually

**Added 2026-08-30 in answer to "how clean is clean".** The 3-sigma test is a yes or
no and the placebo showed it is nearly uninformative, so the window was placed in the
distribution of **every** window of the same shape in the series, 2,094 of them. They
overlap, so these are percentiles and not independent p-values, which is stated rather
than dressed up.

| statistic | event window | **percentile** | median of all windows |
|---|---|---|---|
| max absolute daily change | 1.00 bp | **30.3** (+8.5 tied) | 2.00 bp |
| sum of absolute daily changes | 3.00 bp | **44.6** | 3.00 bp |
| range within the window | 3.00 bp | **56.5** | 2.00 bp |

**The event window was an ordinary week.** Total movement sits at the 45th percentile
and the range sits slightly **above** the median. **It was not quiet, and calling it
clean overstated it.**

**What is unusual is the level shift, and only at the longer spans:**

| span | event shift | **percentile of absolute shift** | median absolute shift | p90 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| ±5 d | −0.600 bp | 32.1 | 1.200 | 5.760 |
| ±10 d | +0.100 bp | **6.9** | 1.100 | 5.080 |
| ±20 d | +0.650 bp | 30.6 | 1.300 | 4.650 |
| ±40 d | +0.125 bp | **7.6** | 1.225 | 5.565 |
| **±60 d** | **−0.017 bp** | **0.9** | 1.483 | 5.490 |

**Every span is below the median shift; three of five are in the bottom eight per
cent; the sixty-day shift is smaller than 99 per cent of all comparable shifts in the
series.** The percentiles bounce between 0.9 and 32, which is what one noisy draw
looks like, **so the claim is the consistent direction and not any single figure.**

**So the corrected statement is:** the week itself was ordinary; **what did not happen
is that the basis relocated.** The market kept moving exactly as much as it always
does, and it moved around the same level.

## Two things that weaken it, and the second is serious

**1. The abnormality test has no power and should not be cited.** The placebo, after
flooring the scale at the quotation grid, shows **6 of 20 random dates** also carrying
an abnormal day in their own window. **So a clean event window is a 70 per cent
outcome under the null.** Zero hits is not evidence. **The evidence here is the level
comparison, and only that.**

**The first placebo run was worse and is recorded rather than quietly fixed.** In quiet
windows the MAD of daily changes is exactly zero on a one basis point grid, so any
move is infinitely many sigmas and the test fired on nothing: eight of twenty, with
divide-by-zero warnings. **Flooring the scale at the grid is failure mode 112 turning
up a fourth time, now inside a control rather than inside a result.**

**2. The switch was announced long in advance, so an event study at the implementation
date tests almost nothing.** LCH set out the plan in 2019 and confirmed the date in
mid-2020. **An anticipated repricing appears at the announcement, not at the
implementation.** So "no move on the date" is the expected observation under **both**
hypotheses, and criterion B as registered cannot separate them.

**What criterion B actually established** is narrower and still worth having: **there
was no disruption, no liquidity event and no repricing at the moment 120 trillion
changed valuation basis.** That is a fact about the switch, not a discrimination
between H_taut and H_real.

**The repair, registered rather than run:** the same test at the **announcement**
dates, and on instruments that would have to move if the switch carried information,
which is the SOFR versus Fed Funds basis swap curve rather than the overnight fixings
used here. **Overnight fixings are the wrong instrument for an announcement effect and
that was not thought through before the run.**

## Criterion A remains open

**LCH has not published the total cash compensation.** Without it there is no measure
of how much of the agreed value was the procedure, which is the number this arm was
built to get. **The 24 billion of compensating basis swaps is a transfer of residual
risk and is not the valuation differential**, and the design file forbids substituting
one for the other.

## Limits

1. **The event test is powerless, per above.** Only the level result stands.
2. **Overnight fixings are not the instrument that would carry an announcement
   effect.** This is the arm's main design fault and it is recorded.
3. **CME switched on the same weekend** and is not separated here.
4. **Nothing here reads who paid whom or how much**, which is criterion A.

---

---

# B30-18, chunk two: criterion A is not disclosed anywhere, the magnitude is computable instead, and the arm picked the smaller of the two available switches

**Read 2026-08-31.** §4.10's criterion A asks for the total cash compensation of the
2020 discounting switch, which would measure directly **how much of an agreed value is
the procedure.**

## No party published it. Source classes enumerated first, per D32

| class | tried | result |
|---|---|---|
| CCP press release | yes | LCH gives notional, contracts and compensating-swap notional. **No cash total.** |
| CCP methodology whitepaper | yes | CME's is a method document. **Account-level illustrations only**, one hypothetical with 2,903,960 dollars of gross discounting DV01 and a 100 million dollar hypothetical portfolio. **No aggregate.** |
| regulator filing | yes | The CFTC MRAC appendix gives the formula and no total. |
| law-firm and trade summaries | yes | Process only. |
| practitioner forum | yes | Claims only, recorded below and not used. |
| bank annual reports and 10-K | **no** | untried |
| CPMI-IOSCO quantitative disclosures | **no** | untried |

**Two classes remain untried, so the figure is not declared unobtainable.** But no
central counterparty published it, and that is now established rather than assumed.

## The magnitude is computable, and that is better than a disclosure would have been

Hull and White, *OIS Discounting, Interest Rate Derivatives, and the Modeling of
Stochastic Interest Rate Spreads*, Journal of Investment Management 13(1), 2015,
assuming **OIS swap rates equal LIBOR swap rates less 100 basis points**, give the
value change of a pay-fixed swap struck 1 per cent away from market when the discount
curve is switched from LIBOR to OIS, **per unit of notional**:

| swap life | 4-to-6 curve | flat curve | 6-to-4 curve |
|---|---|---|---|
| 10 years | −39.6 bp | −38.1 bp | −36.7 bp |
| 20 years | −114.5 bp | −112.6 bp | −110.8 bp |
| 30 years | −182.0 bp | −192.6 bp | −204.0 bp |

**A twenty-year seasoned swap changes value by about 1.1 per cent of its notional
because the discounting convention changed. Nothing about the contract, the
counterparties or the world changed.**

**That is criterion A's quantity, obtained analytically rather than from a
disclosure**, and it is the clearest single number this chain has produced for the
question "how much of an agreed price is the procedure".

## And it exposes a design fault: the arm picked the smaller switch

**The value transfer scales with the spread between the two conventions.**

```
2010  LIBOR discounting -> OIS discounting     spread of order 10 to 40 bp and more
2020  Fed Funds discounting -> SOFR            basis measured in chunk one at -0.4 bp
```

**So the 2020 switch moved value by roughly one part in a hundred of what the 2010
switch moved, because the two curves it swapped between were nearly the same curve.**

**B30-18 chose the switch where there was almost nothing at stake.** Chunk one's
criterion B result is unaffected, since it asks whether the market relocated and not
how much value moved. **But criterion A cannot be answered on the 2020 event and
should be re-registered on the 2010 one**, where LCH switched SwapClear to OIS
discounting and the spread was large.

**This is a carrier-selection fault and it was visible before the run**: the design
file's own §4.10 quoted the compensating-swap notional at 24 billion against 120
trillion of cleared notional, a ratio of 0.02 per cent, **and that ratio was the
warning that the two conventions nearly coincided.** It was written down and not read.

## The practitioner claims, recorded and not used

A trading forum thread on the 2009 to 2013 LIBOR-to-OIS transition carries a claim
that one desk **"netted around 9 yards over those 4 years"**, and a second-hand
reference to **BNP Paribas taking a 108 million euro loss**, attributed there to Risk.

**Anonymous forum claims, and one second-hand attribution.** They are recorded because
they point at the right event and the right order of magnitude, and they are **not
used for anything**. The BNP figure is worth chasing to its source because a
bank-level disclosed figure would be criterion A at the level of one book.

## What stands after both chunks

1. **Criterion B, from chunk one:** across the 2020 switch the traded basis between the
   two curves did not relocate, at every window from five days to sixty, with the
   sixty-day shift smaller than 99 per cent of comparable shifts. **Weakened by the
   switch having been announced long in advance.**
2. **Criterion A, from this chunk:** not disclosed by any CCP; **computable at about
   1.1 per cent of notional for a seasoned twenty-year swap at a 100 basis point
   convention spread**; and **the 2020 carrier was the wrong one to ask it on.**
3. **Re-registration needed:** criterion A on the 2010 LCH switch, and criterion B on
   announcement dates using basis swap curves rather than overnight fixings.

---

---

# B30-18, chunk three: nobody ever published the number, and the same cash flows were priced differently by every bank

**Read 2026-08-31.** Two remaining source classes for criterion A were tried, and the
search turned up something better than the figure it was looking for.

## Criterion A: the number does not exist in public, from anyone

| class | result |
|---|---|
| CCP press release, whitepaper, regulator filing | **no aggregate**, chunk two |
| bank annual reports and disclosures | **searched, nothing found.** The one figure in circulation, a 108 million euro BNP Paribas loss, traces only to a forum post citing a paywalled trade article and **could not be taken to a primary source** |
| academic literature | **searched.** Spears (2019), the standard scholarly account of the episode, **documents the transition in detail and reports no bank-level or aggregate valuation impact** |

**So: a change of convention that moved value on the order of one per cent of notional
across hundreds of trillions of dollars of contracts was never quantified in public by
any counterparty, any bank, any regulator or any scholar.** That absence is now
established across six source classes and it is itself the finding for criterion A.

**The only magnitudes available are the analytic one from chunk two and three
anecdotes**: roughly one billion dollars of collateral-arbitrage profit at one bank
(Cameron 2013, via Spears), a claim of nine billion over four years at one desk, and
the untraceable BNP figure. **The first is sourced, the other two are not.**

## What the search found instead, and it is worth more

Taylor Spears, *Discounting Collateral: Quants, Derivatives, and the Reconstruction of
the "Risk-Free Rate" after the Financial Crisis*, **Economy and Society 48(3),
343-370, 2019**, open access, based on interviews with practitioners.

**1. The convention was adopted, not derived.**

> *"quants and traders eventually adopted a new market convention: the value of a
> derivative traded under a simple CSA ... would serve as a benchmark for the market
> price"*

And on how it became standard:

> *"By 2010 the use of OIS discounting had become sufficiently widespread within the
> market that Risk had declared the practice a new 'market standard', even though it
> had not yet been fully adopted by all banks."*

**A trade magazine declaring a practice standard is the mechanism by which the
benchmark became the benchmark.** That is the propagation rule from
[`b30_propagation.md`](b30_propagation.md) in one sentence, in a market where the
number is a valuation rather than a posted price.

**2. The same cash flows carried different prices at different banks, and the paper
says why.**

> *"a client requesting a valuation for an uncollateralized derivative from a set of
> dealer banks would be given a range of valuations depending on the financial
> characteristics of the bank valuing the instrument"*

> *"a market participant requesting a price for even a simple derivative will receive
> a different price from each bank depending on the precise features of the existing
> CSA"*

**Identical contractual cash flows, several simultaneous prices, and the dispersion is
produced by the collateral agreement and the valuing bank's own funding, not by any
disagreement about the world.** That is [`b30`](b30_prereg.md)'s two-prices object,
D30, appearing in the most mathematically sophisticated market there is.

**3. A trade body then wrote a standard to collapse the dispersion, with a date.**

> *"In 2013, ISDA developed and released what became known as the 'Standard CSA',
> which as its name suggests, simplifies and standardizes the original CSA."*

> *"Throughout 2016-2017, banks attempted to force their clients to adopt standardized
> CSAs as a part of an effort to meet new post-crisis regulations"*

**A third party who is neither buyer nor seller published a standard whose function is
to make different parties' valuations of the same cash flows agree.** That is the same
operator as B30-8's created edge and the ATPCO decree's deleted edges, in a third
market and on a third date.

## Why this matters more than the missing figure

**The chain has been trying to show that agreement between parties can be manufactured
by a shared procedure rather than found in the world.** Here it is with the sign
reversed and therefore harder to explain away:

> **Before the convention was shared, the same cash flows had several prices at once,
> and the spread between them was set by which bank was asked and under what
> collateral terms. A trade body then published a standard to remove it.**

**Nothing in that requires anyone to be wrong, uninformed or irrational**, which is the
scope condition [`b30_prereg.md`](b30_prereg.md) §4 imposes on every arm.

## Limits

1. **Spears is an interview-based account.** The quoted sentences are Spears's own
   characterisation of practice, corroborated by interviews, **not measurements.** No
   dispersion figure is given, so "a range of valuations" has no width attached.
2. **Nothing here is a preregistered reading.** This chunk is a source find made while
   pursuing criterion A, and it scores against nothing. **It is registered as the
   carrier for a future arm, not as a result.**
3. **The BNP figure remains untraced** and is not used.
4. **Criterion A stands unclosed** and is re-registered on the 2010 switch in the
   design file.

---

---

# B30-18, chunk four: the 2010 circular. The convention was not switched once, it was maintained weekly in cash

**Read 2026-08-31** on the carrier criterion A was re-registered to in §4.10a: LCH's
2010 move from LIBOR to OIS discounting. **Primary document found: the member
circular itself.**

## The document

LCH.Clearnet member circular, **16 June 2010**. The clearing house will use

> *"OIS rate curves to discount its SwapClear portfolio"*

and

> *"The first one-off adjustment will be communicated on Friday 25 June 2010 with the
> postings occurring for value Tuesday 29 June 2010."*

**No total is given here either**, so criterion A's figure remains unpublished across
seven source classes now. **But the mechanism the circular sets out is worth more than
the figure.**

## What the mechanism says, and it is not what the 2020 event suggested

> *"Every week LCH.Clearnet Risk will recalculate the Discount Risk from USD, EUR and
> GBP across the SwapClear membership and will derive the adjustment required (debit
> or credit) for each member. This amount will be communicated via email to all
> members every Friday. The amount will be debited or credited to each members'
> currency Buffer T account for value the following Tuesday."*

**This is not a one-off compensation followed by a return to normal. It is a standing
weekly cash transfer between members, in three currencies, whose size is set by
nothing except which curve the clearing house discounts at.**

LCH's own term for the quantity is **"Discount Risk"**, and the circular states that
**material** Discount Risk arises in USD, EUR and GBP.

**So the shared procedure has a maintenance cost.** It is not a coordinate that is
chosen once and then costs nothing to hold. **Keeping every member consistent with it
required recomputing the discrepancy every week and moving cash between them every
Tuesday.**

## Why this is a better answer to criterion A than the missing total

Criterion A asked **how much of an agreed value is the procedure**. A single
compensation figure would have answered it for one instant. **The circular answers it
structurally and continuously:**

> **The gap between what the contract is worth under one convention and under another
> was large enough, and moved enough, that a clearing house metered it weekly and
> settled it in cash between its members for as long as the two curves differed.**

**And it explains the 2020 event's shape.** By 2020 the two curves being swapped
differed by −0.4 basis points, so the switch was a single auction with a one-off
adjustment and 24 billion of compensating swaps, and nothing standing was needed. **In
2010 the difference was large and live, so the arrangement had to be a standing one.
The same operator, two decades apart, with the size of the convention gap setting
whether it needs continuous maintenance.**

## What is established

1. **The 2010 switch is dated to the day**: circular 2010-06-16, first adjustment
   communicated 2010-06-25, cash for value 2010-06-29.
2. **The transfer was ongoing, not one-off**, recalculated weekly across the whole
   membership in three currencies.
3. **The clearing house named the quantity** and called it material.
4. **The total is still unpublished**, now across seven classes.

## Limits

1. **No figures at all in the circular.** Everything quantitative for this carrier is
   still missing, and Hull and White's analytic table from chunk two remains the only
   magnitude in hand.
2. **The circular is a fetched archive page on LCH's old member-notice host.** It was
   not cross-checked against a second copy, and the host is a legacy one.
3. **How long the weekly arrangement ran is not established.** If it was folded into
   ordinary variation margin later, that date is not known here.
4. **Nothing here reads who paid whom, or the sign or dispersion across members**,
   which is what would turn "material" into a number.

---

---

# B30-18, chunk five: criterion B cannot be identified on this carrier, and the reason is worth more than another run

**Read 2026-08-31.** §4.10a re-registered criterion B on the **announcement** date
rather than the implementation date, on the ground that an anticipated repricing shows
up when it is announced. **Dating the announcement killed the repair.**

## There is no announcement date

LCH letter to all SwapClear users, **2019-07-26**, from the Head of SwapClear and the
Head of Risk, Rates Services:

> *"This announcement was made following a broad consultation process which took place
> at the end of last year."*

> *"We are targeting the conversion to take place on or around 17th October 2020"*

with the auction one to two days before. And the letter refers to a plan LCH
*"announced ... earlier this year"*.

**So the information came out in at least four stages over two years:**

```
end of 2018     a broad consultation with members
early 2019      the plan announced
2019-07-26      this letter, targeting on or around 2020-10-17
2020 mid-year   the date confirmed, mechanics published
2020-10-16/19   auction and implementation
```

**No single date carries the news.** An event study needs a moment at which the market
learns something, and this switch was designed with its counterparties, in public,
across two years, with the compensation scheme worked out in advance.

## So criterion B is closed as unidentifiable, on three grounds

1. **Implementation date**: anticipated, so no move is expected under either
   hypothesis. Chunk one already said this.
2. **Announcement date**: does not exist as a point. Four staged releases, none of them
   a surprise.
3. **Announcement window**: 2018 end to 2020 October is confounded beyond repair.
   SOFR was a new rate still building history, the September 2019 repo spike sits in
   the middle of it, and COVID sits at the end. **The design file requires reporting
   unidentified when confounds cannot be excluded, and here they cannot.**

**The arm returns unidentified on criterion B and it is recorded as unidentified, not
as a pass.** Chunk one's level result stands as a description of the switch week and
is not evidence for discriminator two.

## What the staged process is evidence of, labelled as argument

**A number that responds to information does not get changed by a two-year
consultation with a compensation scheme designed in advance and an auction to clear
the residual.** That procedure is what parties build when they need to move everyone
from one coordinate to another **without anyone gaining or losing from the move
itself**. The whole apparatus exists to make the change value-neutral.

**This is an argument from the shape of the machinery, not a measurement**, and it is
labelled so. It is the same class of claim as the SOFR fallback's fixed historical
spread adjustment: **you can only
bridge two conventions with a pre-agreed constant if the difference between them
carries nothing that has to be discovered.**

## B30-18 closed. What it delivered and what it did not

**Delivered.**

1. **The operator, stated by the party.** Cash flows held constant, procedure changed,
   compensation equals the difference in present value. LCH's own filing.
2. **The magnitude, analytically.** About **1.1 per cent of notional** for a seasoned
   twenty-year swap at a 100 basis point convention spread (Hull and White).
3. **The mechanism, from the 2010 circular.** Not a one-off: a **weekly** recalculation
   of Discount Risk across the membership in three currencies with cash posted every
   Tuesday. **A shared procedure with a standing maintenance cost.**
4. **The dependence on the convention gap.** 2010 needed a standing arrangement; 2020,
   at a gap of −0.4 basis points, needed one auction.
5. **A negative that is now solid**: the total was never published by anyone, across
   **seven** source classes.

**Not delivered.**

1. **Criterion B**, unidentifiable, above.
2. **Any aggregate or per-bank figure.**
3. **Any separation of H_taut from H_real.** **Discriminator two has not in fact been
   run.** The carrier turned out not to admit the test, and that is the arm's honest
   summary.

**The next carrier for discriminator two must be chosen on one condition that this one
failed: the procedure change has to be a surprise, or at least have a datable moment
at which the market learned of it.** A negotiated industry migration never will be.
**Registered as the selection rule for the next attempt.**

---

---

# B30-19, chunk one: eighteen banks adopted one valuation convention over thirteen quarters, and every one of them booked a loss doing it

**Read 2026-08-31** against §4.11's criterion A, registered the same day. **This is the
figure seven source classes failed to produce for the discounting switch.**

## The primary quote

J.P. Morgan Chase, fourth-quarter 2013 earnings release, **2014-01-14**, filed with the
SEC:

> *"Net revenue included a **$1.5 billion loss** as a result of implementing a funding
> valuation adjustment ("FVA") framework for OTC derivatives and structured notes.
> **This change reflects an industry migration towards incorporating the cost or
> benefit of funding into their valuation**; the majority of this adjustment relates to
> uncollateralized derivatives."*

**A bank booked one and a half billion dollars of loss because the industry migrated
to a different valuation convention.** Positions unchanged, counterparties unchanged,
cash flows unchanged. **The reason given, by the bank, in an SEC filing, is "an
industry migration."**

## The table

Andersen, Duffie and Song, *Funding Value Adjustments*, NBER Working Paper 23680,
August 2017, published in the Journal of Finance, Table I:

| bank | amount | disclosed |
|---|---|---|
| Goldman Sachs | unknown | **Q4 2011** |
| Deutsche Bank | €364 m | Q4 2012 |
| Royal Bank of Scotland | $475 m | Q4 2012 |
| Barclays | £101 m | Q4 2012 |
| Lloyds Banking Group | €143 m | Q4 2012 |
| **J.P. Morgan Chase** | **$1,500 m** | **Q4 2013** |
| ANZ | A$61 m | Q4 2013 |
| Bank of Ireland | €36 m | Q4 2013 |
| Nomura | $98 m | Q1 2014 |
| BNP Paribas | €166 m | Q2 2014 |
| Crédit Agricole | €167 m | Q2 2014 |
| UBS | Fr267 m | Q3 2014 |
| Crédit Suisse | Fr279 m | Q3 2014 |
| Bank of America Merrill Lynch | $497 m | Q4 2014 |
| Citi | $474 m | Q4 2014 |
| Morgan Stanley | $468 m | Q4 2014 |
| HSBC | $263 m | Q4 2014 |
| Royal Bank of Canada | C$105 m | Q4 2014 |

**Roughly five to six billion dollars in total**, converting the non-dollar amounts at
approximate contemporaneous rates and with Goldman unknown. **The total is stated as an
order of magnitude and not as a figure**, because the conversion is mine and the paper
does not sum the column.

## Four readings

**1. Criterion A is answered, and this is the chain's first disclosed measurement of
what a convention is worth.** Not a model output, not an anecdote: eighteen separate
regulatory filings.

**2. The charges are all in one direction.** Every entry is a loss. **A pure change of
coordinate would be zero-sum across the parties; this was not.** Adopting the
convention made every adopter poorer on paper at once, which means the quantity being
added was not previously anywhere in anyone's books.

**3. The adoption is spread over thirteen quarters, and it clusters on fourth
quarters.** Q4 2011, then four banks in Q4 2012, three in Q4 2013, five in Q4 2014.
**During those three years the same uncollateralised derivative was carried at
materially different values by banks that had adopted and banks that had not.** That
is Spears's *"a different price from each bank"* with dates and amounts attached.

**4. The leading academic treatment says the adjustment does not belong in market
values at all.** Andersen, Duffie and Song:

> *"FVAs are not themselves components of the market values of the positions being
> financed. [They are] debt-overhang costs to their shareholders."*

**So eighteen banks put into their reported market values a quantity that the standard
scholarly analysis says is not part of market value, and paid several billion dollars
of disclosed losses to do it.**

## What this does NOT establish, and the paper says so itself

**The copying reading is not supported by the paper's own account of causes.**
Andersen, Duffie and Song attribute adoption to elevated post-2008 borrowing costs and
to **acceptance by the accounting firms**, and **no statement in the paper says any
bank adopted because a competitor had.** **The temporal clustering is suggestive and
it is not evidence**, because the fourth-quarter concentration has an obvious
alternative cause: **that is when auditors sign off.**

**And that alternative is the more interesting hypothesis, not the boring one.** If the
convention propagated through the audit function, then the shared procedure is the
auditor rather than the peer bank. **Same structure, different third party**, and it
puts the accounting firms in the position ISDA held for the Standard CSA and Risk
magazine held for OIS discounting.

## Registered as the next reading, before it is looked at

**Do banks sharing an auditor adopt in the same quarter?** Audit assignments for these
eighteen banks are public and the adoption quarters are in the table above.

```
adoption quarter clusters by audit firm   -> the auditor is the shared procedure
no relation to audit firm                 -> the clustering is the annual cycle alone,
                                             and the propagation channel is unfound
```

**The table is already in hand, so the audit assignments must be collected without
looking at the crossing until both columns exist.** Stated here so that the order is
on the record.

## Limits

1. **The table is Andersen, Duffie and Song's, not this project's collection.** It has
   not been checked back to the eighteen filings. **One was checked**, the JPMorgan
   figure, against the SEC document itself.
2. **Goldman's amount is unknown**, so the earliest adopter has no number.
3. **The currency conversion is mine and approximate**, which is why no exact total is
   given.
4. **The charges are first-time adoption effects only.** Ongoing FVA is not measured
   here and is much larger cumulatively.
5. **Confounds the design file requires be excluded are not excluded**: Basel III,
   each bank's own funding-cost path, and the litigation and restructuring one-offs of
   those years. **Criterion A is a collection, not a causal reading**, and nothing here
   claims otherwise.
